Decidability of Graph Neural Networks via Logical Characterizations
Michael Benedikt and Chia-Hsuan Lu and Tony Tan

Abstract
We present results concerning the expressiveness and decidability of a popular graph learning formalism, graph neural networks (GNNs), exploiting connections with logic. We use a family of recently-discovered decidable logics involving “Presburger quantifiers”. We show how to use these logics to measure the expressiveness of classes of GNNs, in some cases getting exact correspondences between the expressiveness of logics and GNNs. We also employ the logics, and the techniques used to analyze them, to obtain decision procedures for verification problems over GNNs. We complement this with undecidability results for static analysis problems involving the logics, as well as for GNN verification problems.



1
Introduction

Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have become the most common model for learning functions that work on graph data.
Like traditional neural networks, GNNs consist of a layered architecture where layer
k+1
takes as input the output
of layer
k
. Each layer computes a function from graph vertices to a vector of numerical values – the

feature vector
. Computation of the feature vector at layer
k+1
for a node
u
is based on aggregating vectors
for layer
k
of nodes
v
that are related to
u
in the source graph:
for example aggregating vectors associated
to nodes adjacent to
u
in the graph. In an aggregation, the vectors of the previous vectors may be transformed using linear functions.
A layer can perform multiple aggregations – corresponding to different linear functions – and then combine them to get the feature vector for the next layer. The use of graph structure ensures that the computation of the network is
invariant
: depending only on the input graph and the node up to isomorphism.
There are many variations of GNN. One key design choice is the kind of aggregation used - one can use “local aggregation”, over the neighbors of a node, or aggregation over all nodes in the graph.
A second design choice is the kind of numerical functions that can be applied to vector components, in particular the kind of
activation functions
that can be applied at each layer: e.g.
\mathsf{ReLU}
, sigmoid, piecewise linear functions.

An important issue in the study of graph learning is the
expressiveness
of a learning model. What kinds of computations can a given type of GNN express?
The first results in this line were about the
separating power of a graph learning model
: what pairs of nodes can be distinguished using GNNs within a certain class. For example, it is known that the separating power of standard GNN models is limited by the Weisfeiler-Leman (WL) test
[
22
]
.

A finer-grained classification would characterize the functions computed by GNNs within a certain class, in terms of some formalism that is easier to analyze. Such characterizations are referred to as
uniform expressiveness results
and there has been much less work in this area.

[
2
]
provides a classification of a class of GNNs in terms of
modal logic
. The main result in
[
2
]
is a characterization of the classifiers expressible in first-order logic that can be performed with a GNN having only
local aggregation
and
truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
activations
over
undirected graphs
.
They also provide a lower bound on the expressiveness of GNNs having in addition a “global aggregator”, that sums over all nodes in the graph.

In this work we continue the line of work on uniform expressiveness.
Our work improves on the state of the art in a number of directions:

•

From first order expressiveness to general expressiveness
In contrast to
[
2
]
, we provide logical characterizations of
all
the functions that can be computed by certain GNN formalisms, not just the intersection with first-order logic. To do this we utilize logics that go beyond first order, but which are still amenable to analysis.

•

From expressiveness to verification
While we deal with GNNs that go beyond first-order logic, we can still obtain characterizations in a logic where the basic satisfiability problems are decidable. This provides us with decidability of a number of natural verification problems related to GNNs. In doing this, we show a surprising link between GNNs and recently-devised decidable logics going beyond first-order logic, so-called
Presburger logics
.

•

From undirected graphs to directed graphs
While prior work focused on undirected graphs, we explore how the expressiveness characterizations vary with GNNs that can recognize directionality of graph edges. The aim is to show that the logical characterizations and decidability are often independent of the restriction to undirected graphs.

•

From bounded to unbounded activations
We explore the impact of the activation functions.
We begin with the case of
bounded activation functions
, like the truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
of
[
2
]
, and establish characterizations and decidability results for GNNs using this function. We show both some contrasts and some similarity to the case of
unbounded activation functions
, including the standard
\mathsf{ReLU}
. Here some, but not all, of the corresponding decidability results fail.

Organization.
After overviewing related work in Section
2
, we formalize our GNN model and the basic logics we study in Section
3
. In Section
4
, we start with results on logical characterizations of GNNs with “bounded activation functions” – like the truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
of
[
2
]
. We apply these characterizations to get decidability results. In Section
5
we obtain tight complexity bounds for verification problems specifically for the case of GNNs with local aggregation and truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
activations.

Section
6
turns to the case of unbounded activation functions, which includes the traditional
\mathsf{ReLU}
function. Here we provide lower bounds for expressiveness, and then turn to the implications for decidability.
Section
7
gives conclusions and discusses several open issues.

2
Related work

Our work relates to three areas: logical characterizations of GNNs, verification of GNNs, and
decidable logics. We situate our contributions with respect to prior work in these areas below.

Logical characterizations of GNN expressiveness.
Logics have been used to characterized the separating power of GNN languages (“non-uniform expressiveness”) for a number of years: that is, graphs are distinguishable by a class of GNNs if and only if they are distinguisable in a certain logic. See
[
10
]
for an overview. There is much less work on “uniform expressiveness”: characterizing the behaviour of GNNs on all graphs. The direct inspiration for our work is
[
2
]
, which provides results characterizing the formulas within first-order logic that are expressible with GNNs. The recent
[
11
]
provides logical characterizations of GNNs with piecewise linear activations. The logic is not decidable; indeed our undecidability results imply that one cannot capture such GNNs with a decidable logic.
The recent
[
4
]
gives characterizations of rich classes of GNNs using real circuits – again, this does not yield any decidability.
.

Verification of GNNs.
We employ logical characterizations to gain insight on two basic verification problems – whether a given classification can be achieved on some nodes or on all nodes. There is prior work on verification of GNNs, but it focuses on more complex (but arguably more realistic) problems, adversarial robustness. The closest paper to ours is the recent

[
20
]
, which formalizes a broad set of problems related to verifying that the output is in a certain region in Euclidean space.
[
20
]
provides both decidability and undecidability theorems, but they are incomparable to ours both in the results and in the techniques. For example Theorem 1 of
[
20
]
shows undecidability of a satisfiability problem where we verify that certain nodes output a particular value, over GNNs which always distinguish a node from its neighbor. Theorem 2 of
[
20
]
shows a decidability result with a different kind of specification, where the degree of input graphs is bounded.

Independently from our work,
[
18
]
looked at similar verification problems to ours, but for a more restricted class of GNNs: those with truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
and integer coefficients. They prove a
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness result, very similar to our Theorem
5.1
.

Logics combining graph structure with Presburger arithmetic.

An early work on combining logic on interpreted structure with Presburger arithmetic
is
[
13
]
. There the uninterpreted structure consists only of unary predicates, so decidability is much simpler. The formalisms we study are closely related to
[
5
,
15
]
. We rely on one decidability results from

[
5
]
, but we will need to refine the analysis to get our complexity results.

Recently, logics that combine uninterpreted relations with Presburger arithmetic have been applied to the analysis of transformers – transducers that process strings
[
7
,
3
]
. Since this is outside of the context of general graphs, the details of the logics that are employed are a bit different than those we consider, and the focus is not on the decidability border.

3
Preliminaries

Let
\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathbb{Q}
be the set of natural numbers, positive natural numbers, integers, and rational numbers, respectively.
For
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
with
p\leq q
,
{\left[p\mathinner{,}\penalty q\right]}
is the set of integers between
p
and
q
, including
p
and
q
. When
p
is
1
, we omit it and simply write
{\left[q\right]}
.
For
r\in\mathbb{Q}
,
\left\lceil r\right\rceil
is the smallest integer greater than or equal to
r
, and

\left\lfloor r\right\rfloor
is the greatest integer less than or equal to
r
.

For a function
f
mapping from
\mathbb{Q}
to
\mathbb{Q}
and a vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}
,
f(\mathbf{v})
denotes that
f
is applied to each entry of
\mathbf{v}
.

Definition 3.1
.

An
n
-graph
is a tuple
\left\langle V,E,\left\{U_{c}\right\}_{c\in{\left[n\right]}}\right\rangle
,
where
n\in\mathbb{N}
is the number of vertex colors;

V
is a nonempty finite set of vertices;

E\subseteq V\times V
is a set of edges;
each
U_{c}\subseteq V
is the set of
c
-colored vertices.

Note that we allow self-loops in graphs, and a graph is by default a
directed graph
.
For a graph
\mathcal{G}
, we say that
\mathcal{G}
is a
undirected graph
if for all
v,u\in V
,
(v,u)\in E
if and only if
(u,v)\in E
.
For a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
, we let

{\mathcal{N}_{\text{out},\mathcal{G}}(v):=\left\{u\middle|\ (v,u)\in E\right\}}
and refer to this as the set of
out-neighbors of
v
.
The set of
in-neighbors of
v
, denoted

\mathcal{N}_{\text{in},\mathcal{G}}(v)
are defined analogously.

In the body of the paper, we will present results about GNNs that work over directed graphs. Some of our results will make heavy use of directionality, considering GNNs that only look at out-neighbors. But many results will deal with “bidirectional GNNs”, those that can see both out- and in-neighbors.
In the appendix
we show that the results which will deal with bidirectional GNNs also apply to undirected graphs
.

Graph neural networks.

We use a standard notion of “aggregate-combine” graph neural networks with rational coefficients. The only distinction from the usual presentation is that
we allow GNNs to work over directed graphs, with separate aggregations over incoming and outgoing edges, while traditional GNNs work on undirected graphs.

Definition 3.2
.

An
n
-graph neural network
(GNN) is a tuple

\left\langle\left\{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty L\right]}},\left\{f^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}},\left\{\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}},\left\{\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\left[L\right]}\\
x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}\end{subarray}},\left\{\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}},\left\{\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}\right\rangle,

where
L\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
is the number of layers;
each
{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
, called the
dimension
of the
\ell^{th}
layer, requiring
{d^{\left(0\right)}}:=n
, the number of colors;
each
{f^{\left(\ell\right)}:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}}
, the
activation function
of the
\ell^{th}
layer;
each
{\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)},\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x},\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\in\mathbb{Q}^{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}\times{d^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}}
, the
coefficient matrices
of the
\ell^{th}
layer; and

\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\in\mathbb{Q}^{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}}
, the
bias vector
of the
\ell^{th}
layer.

All the coefficients are rational. In order to have an effective representation of a GNN, we will also
assume that the activation functions are computable
.

Definition 3.3
.

For an
n
-GNN
\mathcal{A}
and an
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
,
the
computation of
\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{G}
is a sequence of
derived feature functions

\left\{\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}:V\to\mathbb{Q}^{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty L\right]}}
defined inductively.
For
v\in V
,
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v)
is called the
\ell
-feature (vector) of
v
, and

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathcal{G},i}(v)
denotes the
i^{th}
entry of
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v)
.

For
\ell=0
, for
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(0\right)}}
,
if
v\in U_{i}
, then
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{\mathcal{G},i}(v)=1
; otherwise,
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{\mathcal{G},i}(v)=0
.
For
1\leq\ell\leq L
,

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v):=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v)+\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}}\!\!\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{x,\mathcal{G}}(v)}\!\!\!\!\!\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(u)\right)+\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(u)+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right).

That is, we compute the feature values of a node
v
at layer
\ell+1
by adding several components. One component aggregates over the
\ell
-layer feature vector from the outgoing neighbors of
v
, and applies a affine transformation. Another component does the same for the incoming neighbors of
v
, a third does this for every node in the graph, while another applies a affine transformation to the
\ell
-layer feature vector of
v
itself. The affine transformation can be different for each component, and in particular can be a zero matrix that just drops that component. The final component of the sum is the bias vector.

When the graph
\mathcal{G}
is clear from the context,
we omit it and simply write
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)
and
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{i}(v)
, and similarly
when the graph
\mathcal{G}
is clear from the context,
write
\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)
and
\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(v)
for the in-neighbors and out-neighbors.

Note that in most presentations of GNNs, one deals with only undirected edges. The above definition degenerates in that setting to two aggregations per layer, with the aggregation over all nodes
often referred to in the literature as the
global readout
.

In some presentations of GNNs, a
classification function
, which associates a final Boolean decision to a node, is included in the definition. In our case, we have separated out the classification function as an independent component in defining the expressiveness: see the last part of the preliminaries.

Classes of activation functions.

Following prior work on analysis of GNNs, some of our results will deal with activation functions that are bounded in value:

Definition 3.4
.

We say that the function
f:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}

is
eventually constant
,
if there exists
t_{\mathsf{left}},t_{\mathsf{right}}\in\mathbb{Q}

satisfying
t_{\mathsf{left}}<t_{\mathsf{right}}
,
called the
left and right thresholds of
f
,
such that
for every
x\leq t_{\mathsf{left}}
,
f(x)=f(t_{\mathsf{left}})
;
for every
x\geq t_{\mathsf{right}}
,
f(x)=f(t_{\mathsf{right}})
.

A standard eventually constant function is the
truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
function
,
denoted by
\mathsf{TrReLU}
,
which is
0
for negatives,
1
for
x
greater than
1
, and
x
otherwise
[
2
]
.
There are other eventually constant functions that are used in practice: for example, the linear approximation of standard bounded functions used in graph learning, like the Sigmoid activation function. We will be interested in functions that are defined on the reals, but which preserve the rationals. The definition of eventually constant extends to such a function in the obvious way.

For a GNN with eventually constant activation functions,
we use
t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
and
t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}

to denote the left and right thresholds of the GNN’s activation functions.

We also consider
unbounded activation functions
, such as the
standard
\mathsf{ReLU}
function
, which is
x
for non-negatives and
0
for negatives.

Flavors of GNN.

Definition 3.5
.

For a GNN
\mathcal{A}
,
we say that

\mathcal{A}
is
outgoing-only
,
if for every
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{in}}
is a zero matrix.
We say that

\mathcal{A}
is
local
,
if for every
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}
is a zero matrix.

We use the following naming,

{(\mathcal{O})(\mathcal{L})(\mathcal{C}|\mathcal{PW}|\mathsf{TrReLU}|\mathsf{ReLU})\text{-}\mathsf{GNN}}
,
for the set of GNNs satisfying constraints given by the prefix,
where
\mathcal{O}
for outgoing-only;

\mathcal{L}
for local;

\mathcal{C}
for eventually constant activation;

\mathcal{PW}
for
\emph{piecewiselinear}
activation.
For example,
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
is the set of outgoing-only and local GNNs with eventually constant activations;

\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
is the set of GNNs with piecewise linear activations.

Classifiers and Boolean semantics.

Our GNNs define vector-valued classification functions on nodes. But for comparing with expressiveness and in defining verification problems, we will often use a derived function from nodes to Booleans.
We do this by thresholding at the end – below we use
1/2
for convenience, but other choices do not impact the results.

Definition 3.6
.

For an
n
-GNN
\mathcal{A}
,
an
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
,
and a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
we say that
\mathcal{A}

accepts
the tuple
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,
if
\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{\mathcal{G},1}(v)\geq 1/2
.

Note that the global readout component
can interact with the activation functions
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
, which can behave very differently on translated values due to non-linearity – think of a typical
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
as a piece-wise linear function.
Global readout can also interact with the classification threshold, pushing some values above the threshold while leaving others below.

Satisfaction of logical formulas.

We will deal with various classes of logical formulas on graphs,
\phi(v_{1}\ldots v_{n})
. These will be evaluated on a graph with respect to a graph
G
and a variable binding
\sigma
that maps each free variable to a node of

G
. We often write
G,\sigma\models\phi
, or
G,c_{1}\ldots c_{n}
for nodes
c_{i}
, where the ordering of nodes is understood. For our formulas, we will always have
n
at most
2
.
We will often omit the input graph since it will be obvious from context.

We will also deal logical formulas
over integers
\Psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, which are evaluated with respect to a variable binding
\sigma
mapping free variables to integers. We write
\Psi(\sigma)
or
\Phi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
to denote satisfaction of such a
\Psi
on binding
\sigma

mapping
x_{i}
to
a_{i}
. We sometimes identify the value true with
1
and false with
0
, writing
{\left\llbracket\Psi(\mathbf{a})\right\rrbracket}=1
.

We will use lowercase Greek letters to denote logical formulas over graphs,
and uppercase Greek letters for logical formulas over integers.

Two-variable modal logic with Presburger quantifiers.

We review logic with Presburger quantifiers. The basic idea is to combine a decidable logic on uninterpreted structures, like two-variable logic or guarded logic, with the ability to perform some arithmetic on the number of elements. There are several formalisms in the literature that combine Presburger arithmetic with a decidable uninterpreted logic, some originating many years ago
[
13
]
. We will rely on a recent logic from
[
5
]
, but we will need several variations of the underlying idea here.

Definition 3.7
.

A
Presburger quantifier
is of the form:

\mathcal{P}(x)\ :=\ \sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right]}\ \circledast\ \delta,

where
\delta\in\mathbb{Z}
;
each
\lambda_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}
;
each
\varphi_{t}(x,y)
is a formula with free variables
x
and
y
;

\circledast
is one of
=
,
\neq
,
\leq
,
\geq
,
<
, or
>
.
Note that
\mathcal{P}(x)
has one free variable
x
.

We give the semantics of these quantifiers inductively,
assuming a semantics for
\varphi_{t}(x,y)
.
Given a graph
\mathcal{G}
and a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
we say that
\mathcal{P}(x)
holds in
\mathcal{G},x/v
,
denoted by
{\mathcal{G}\models\mathcal{P}(v)}
,
if the following (in)equality holds in
\mathbb{Z}
:

\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{t}(v,u)\right\}\right|\ \circledast\ \delta.

Remark 3.8
.

Note that each Presburger quantifier
can be rewritten as a Boolean combination of expressions which
only use the inequality symbol
\geq
as
\circledast
.
For example,

{\left({\#_{y}\left[\varphi(x,y)\right]}=\delta\right)}
and

{\left({\#_{y}\left[\varphi(x,y)\right]}\geq\delta\right)}\land\neg{\left({\#_{y}\left[\varphi(x,y)\right]}\geq\delta+1\right)}

are semantically equivalent.
Therefore it is sufficient to consider Presburger quantifiers which only use the inequality symbol
\geq
.

Remark 3.9
.

We will make use of Presburger quantifiers
that allow for rational coefficients of the form:

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(x)\ :=\ \lambda_{0}+\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right]}\ \circledast\ \lambda^{\prime}_{0}+\sum_{t\in{\left[k^{\prime}\right]}}\lambda^{\prime}_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi^{\prime}_{t}(x,y)\right]},

where each
\lambda_{t},\lambda^{\prime}_{t}\in\mathbb{Q}
.
This is a shorthand for the Presburger quantifier:

\mathcal{P}(x)\ :=\ \sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}(D\lambda_{t})\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right]}+\sum_{t\in{\left[k^{\prime}\right]}}(-D\lambda^{\prime}_{t})\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi^{\prime}_{t}(x,y)\right]}\ \circledast\ D(\lambda_{0}-\lambda^{\prime}_{0}),

where
D
is the least common multiplier of the denominators of the coefficients in
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(x)
.

When
\lambda_{t}=1
, we omit it and simply write
{\#_{y}\left[\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right]}
.

Definition 3.10
.

We give the syntax of
two-variable modal logic with Presburger quantifiers
(
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
) over vocabulary
\tau
. Formulas will have exactly one free variable, denoted
x
below.

•

\top
is an
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula.

•

For a unary predicate
U\in\tau
,
U(x)
is an
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula.

•

If
\varphi(x)
is an
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula, then so is
\neg\varphi(x)
.

•

If
\varphi_{1}(x)
and
\varphi_{2}(x)
are
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas, then so is
\varphi_{1}(x)\land\varphi_{2}(x)
.

•

If
\left\{\varphi_{t}(x)\right\}_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}
is a set of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas and

\left\{\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\right\}_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}
is a set of
guard atoms
,
each of form
E(x,y)
,
E(y,x)
, or
\top
, then

\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\land\varphi_{t}(y)\right]}\ \circledast\ \delta

is also an
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula.

\left\{\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\right\}_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}
are the
guards
of the formula. Consistent with the restriction we announced on the logic, we consider the result as a formula with free variable
x
: if all
\epsilon_{i}
are
\top
it returns either every vertex or no vertex.

The semantics of the Boolean connectives is as usual, while the semantics of the Presburger quantifiers is given by Definition
3.7
.

An
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\varphi(x)
is an
n
-formula if its vocabulary consists of
n
unary predicates.
We use abbreviations
\lor
and
\to
as usual.
Note that the guarded universal quantifier

\forall y\ E(x,y)\to\varphi(y)
can be expressed as

\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\neg\varphi(y)\right]}=0\right)
,
and the guarded existential quantifier

\exists y\ E(x,y)\land\varphi(y)
can be expressed as

\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\varphi(y)\right]}\geq 1\right)
.

Definition 3.11
.

For an
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\varphi(x)
,
for a graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
we define the valuation of
\varphi(x)
on
\mathcal{G},v
, denoted by
{\left\llbracket\varphi(v)\right\rrbracket}_{\mathcal{G}}
as follows:

{\left\llbracket\varphi(v)\right\rrbracket}_{\mathcal{G}}\ :=\ \begin{cases}1,&\text{if $\mathcal{G}\models\varphi(v)$}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

When the graph
\mathcal{G}
is clear from the context,
we omit it and simply write
{\left\llbracket\varphi(v)\right\rrbracket}
.

The logic “
local

\mathsf{MP^{2}}
” is obtained by excluding
\top
as a guard.
We say a
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula is
outgoing-only
if there are no guarded atom of form
E(y,x)
.
Analogously to what we did with GNNs, we use
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{O}
to indicate the “local” and ”outgoing-only” fragment of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, respectively.

The logic
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
combines Presburger arithmetic and quantification over the model.
Thus one might worry that it has an undecidable satisfiability problem. And indeed, we will show this: see Theorem
4.23
.
An idea to gain decidability is to impose that the quantification is
guarded
– again, the underlying idea is from
[
5
]
.

The logic
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
is contained in the following logic, defined in
[
5
]
:

Definition 3.12
.

The syntax of the
guarded fragment of two-variable logic with Presburger quantifiers
(
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
) over colored graph vocabulary
\tau
starts with arbitrary atoms over the vocabulary, with the usual connective closure and the following rules for quantifiers.

•

If
\varphi(x)
is a
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
formula,
then so are
\forall x\ \epsilon(x)\to\varphi(x)
and
\exists x\ \epsilon(x)\land\varphi(x)
, where
\epsilon
is either
U(x)
for some unary predicate
U\in\tau
or
x=x
.

•

If
\varphi(x,y)
is a
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
formula,
then so are
\forall x\ \epsilon(x,y)\to\varphi(x,y)
and
\exists x\ \epsilon(x,y)\land\varphi(x,y)
, where
\epsilon(x,y)
is either
E(x,y)
or
E(y,x)
.

•

If
\left\{\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right\}_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}
is a set of
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
formulas and

\left\{\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\right\}_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}
is a set of atoms,
each of form
E(x,y)
or
E(y,x)
,
then

\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\land\varphi_{t}(x,y)\right]}\ \circledast\ \delta

is also a
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
formula.

The main difference between the logic
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and the logic above is that the former is “modal”, restricting to one-variable formulas, and allowing two variables only in the guards. While in the logic above we can build up more interesting two variable formulas, for example conjoining two guards.

We will make use of the following prior decidability result:

Theorem 3.13

(
[
5
]
, Theorem 10)
.

The finite satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
is decidable.

From this we easily derive the decidability of
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
:

Corollary 3.14
.

The finite satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
is decidable.

Proof 3.15
.

Let
\varphi(x)
be an
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula and
U_{n+1}
be a fresh unary predicate.
We claim that
\varphi(x)
is finitely satisfiable if and only if the
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
sentence

\psi:=\exists x\ U_{n+1}(x)\land\varphi(x)
is also finitely satisfiable.
Then the corollary follows from the decidability of the finite satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
by Theorem
3.13
.

If
\varphi(x)
is finitely satisfiable by the
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v\in G
,
let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the
(n+1)
-graph that extended
\mathcal{G}
with
U_{n+1}:=\left\{v\right\}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models U_{n+1}(v)
,
which implies that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\psi
.
Hence
\psi
is finitely satisfiable by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

If
\psi
is finitely satisfiable by the
(n+1)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
,
let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the
n
-graph that restricted
\mathcal{G}
by removing
U_{n+1}
.
By definition, there exists at least one vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\mathcal{G}\models U_{n+1}(v)\land\varphi(v)
,
which implies that
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi(v)
.
Since there is no
U_{n+1}
in
\varphi(x)
,

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\varphi(v)
.
Hence
\varphi(x)
is finitely satisfiable by
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Notions of expressiveness for GNNs and
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas.

Recalling that we have a node-to-Boolean semantics available for both logical formulas and GNNs (via thresholding),
we use the term
n
-specification
for either an
n
-GNN or an
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula.

Definition 3.16
.

If
S_{1},S_{2}
are
n
-GNNs, they are said to be
equivalent
if they accept the same nodes within
n
-graphs.
If
S_{1}
is an
n
-GNN and
S_{2}
a node formula in some logic,
we say
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
are equivalent
if for every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,

S_{1}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
if and only if
\mathcal{G},v
satisfies
S_{2}
.

The notions of two languages of specifications being equally expressive, or equally expressive over undirected graphs, is defined in the obvious way.

Verification problems for GNNs.

We focus on two verification problems.
The first is the most obvious analog of satisfiability for GNNs, whether it accepts some node of some graph:

Definition 3.17
.

For an
n
-GNN
\mathcal{A}
,
we say that
\mathcal{A}
is
satisfiable
,
if there exist an
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
, such that
\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.

We will also consider a variation of the problem which asks whether a GNN accepts every node of some graph:

Definition 3.18
.

For an
n
-GNN
\mathcal{A}
,
we say that
\mathcal{A}
is
universally satisfiable
, if there exist an
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
, such that
for every vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.

Two GNNs are equivalent if they accept the same tuples. Note that, like satisfiability and unlike universal satisfiability, this does not require a quantifier alternation. For brevity we will not state results for equivalence, but
it can easily be seen that both our positive and negative results on satisfiability also apply to equivalence
.

3.1
Summary of results

We summarize our analysis of the complexity and decidability of verification problems for GNNs in Table
3.1
and
3.1
.
In cases where we have only written “decidable’, we have not computed precise bounds: and in the “eventually constant” case we would require more hypotheses in order to compute such bounds.

4
Characterization and decidability of GNNs with eventually constant activation functions

In this section, we only consider GNNs with eventually constant activations.
In Section
4.1
, we establish a key tool to analyzing these GNNs: we show that the set of possible activation values is finite, and one can compute an overapproximation of this set.
We use this for two purposes. First we give a decidability result for GNNs with eventually constant activations and only local aggregation, and then we show that even with global aggregation we get an equivalence of the GNNs in expressiveness with a logic.

In Section
4.2
, we show that the finite satisfiability of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
is undecidable. Using the expressiveness characterization, this will imply that satisfiability problems for global GNNs are undecidable. These results were presented for GNNs and logics on directed graphs.

4.1
Decidability of satisfiability problems for GNNs with eventually constant functions, via logic

We now come to one of the crucial definitions in the paper, the spectrum of a GNN.

Definition 4.1
.

For an
n
-GNN
\mathcal{A}
and
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
the
\ell
-spectrum
of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by
\mathcal{S}^{\left(\ell\right)}
,
is the set
\left\{\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)\middle|\ \text{for every $n$-graph $\mathcal{G}$ and vertex $v$ in $\mathcal{G}$}\right\}
.

That is, the
\ell
-spectrum is the range of the features computed at layer
\ell
, as we range over all input graphs and vertices.
We show that, for a GNN with eventually constant activations, the spectrum is actually finite, and a finite superset is computable:

Theorem 4.2
.

For every
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
and
0\leq\ell\leq L
, the
\ell
-spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
is finite.
We can compute a finite superset of the
\ell
-spectrum from the specification of
\mathcal{A}
.

We give some intuition for the proof. Our effective overapproximation of the spectrum will simulate the computation of the GNN, and will be defined inductively on the layers.
Recall that a GNN is given by
dimensions
\left\{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty L\right]}}
,
activation functions
\left\{f^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}
,
coefficient matrices for transforming the prior node value
\left\{\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}
,
coefficient matrices for local aggregation
\left\{\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}\\
x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}\end{subarray}}
,
coefficient matrices for global readout
\left\{\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}
,
and bias vectors
\left\{\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}
.

We now define our overapproximations inductively as follows:

Definition 4.3
.

For every
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
, for
0\leq\ell\leq L
, the set
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(0\right)}}\ :=

\displaystyle\left\{0,1\right\}^{{d^{\left(0\right)}}}

\displaystyle{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}\ :=

\displaystyle\left\{f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{k}}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)\middle|\ \mathbf{k}\in{\left[p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\mathinner{,}\penalty p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}\right]}^{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}}\right\},

where

p^{\left(\ell\right)}
is the product of the least common denominator of elements in
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
and the least common denominator of coefficients in
\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{out}}
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{in}}
,
\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}
, and
\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}
.

We show that the set
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
overapproximates the
\ell
-spectrum:

Lemma 4.4
.

For every
n
-
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
and
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
for every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
.

It is quite straightforward to see that every element of the spectrum is captured.
It is an overapproximation because different integers that we sum in an inductive step
may not be realized in the same graph.

Proof 4.5
.

The proof is by induction on layers.
The base case
\ell=0
is straightforward.
For the inductive step
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
for every vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
by the induction hypothesis,
there exists
\mathbf{s}(v)\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
such that

{\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}(v)}
.
Let
\mathbf{k}(v)
be defined as:

\mathbf{k}(v)\ :=\ p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}(v)+\sum_{x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}}\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{x}(v)}\mathbf{s}(u)\right)+\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\sum_{u\in V}\mathbf{s}(u)+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right).

It is clear that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)
can be rewritten as

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{k}(v)}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)
.
Recall that
p^{\left(\ell\right)}
is the product of the least common denominator of elements in

{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
and the least common denominator of coefficients in
\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{out}}
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{in}}
,
\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}
, and
\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{k}(v)
is an integer vector.

Since
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
is eventually constant with right threshold
t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
.
We can obtain another vector
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(v)\in{\left[p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\mathinner{,}\penalty p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}\right]}^{{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}}
satisfying that

f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(v)}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{k}(v)}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)

by substituting the elements which are greater than
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
by
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
, and the elements that are less than
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
by
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
.
By the definition of
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,

f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(v)}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,
which implies that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
.

We can show by induction on the number of the layers that the set is finite – regardless of computability of the activation functions!

Lemma 4.6
.

For every
n
-
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
and
0\leq\ell\leq L
,

{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
has finite size.

{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
can be computed if activations in
\mathcal{A}
are computable.

In the inductive step, we have a finite set of rationals, thus some fixed precision. We take some integer linear combinations and we will obtain an infinite set of values, but only finitely many between the left and right thresholds of the eventually constant activations. Thus when we apply the activation functions to these values, we will get a finite set of rational values – since the activation functions map rationals to rationals.

Proof 4.7
.

We prove the lemma by induction on layers.
The base case
\ell=0
is trivial.
For the induction step
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
by the induction hypothesis, the size of the set
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
is finite.
Thus, the least common denominator of elements in
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
is well-defined and finite,
which implies that
p^{\left(\ell\right)}
is also finite.
Because the size of the set
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
is bounded by
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot\left(t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}-t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\right)+1
,
the size of
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
is finite.

It is obvious that we can compute the set
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
recursively, assuming the computability of activations in
\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 4.8
.

The restriction to rational coefficients is crucial in the argument.
Consider the following
1
-layer
1
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.
The dimensions are
{d^{\left(0\right)}}={d^{\left(1\right)}}=1
;
the coefficient matrix
\mathbf{C}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero matrix;

\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(1\right)}_{\text{out}}\right)_{1,1}=\sqrt{2}
;

\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(1\right)}_{\text{in}}\right)_{1,1}=-1
;
the bias vector
\mathbf{b}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero vector.
It is not difficult to see that its
1
-spectrum is

\left\{\mathsf{TrReLU}\left(\sqrt{2}t_{1}-t_{2}\right)\middle|\ t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
,
whose size is infinite since
\sqrt{2}
is irrational.

Remark 4.9
.

Even simple GNNs may have exponential size spectra.
For example, let
\mathcal{A}_{k}
be a
1
-layer
1
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined as follows:
the dimensions are
{d^{\left(0\right)}}={d^{\left(1\right)}}=1
;
the coefficient matrix
\mathbf{C}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero matrix;

\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(1\right)}_{\text{out}}\right)_{1,1}=1/k
;
the bias vector
\mathbf{b}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero vector.
By definition, its
1
-spectrum is
\left\{t/k\middle|\ t\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty k\right]}\right\}
, whose size is
k+1
. But the description of
\mathcal{A}_{k}
is only linear in
\log k
.

We now give several applications of the spectrum result.
First we can use the finiteness of the spectrum to get a characterization of the expressiveness of
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
and logic:

Theorem 4.10
.

For every

n
-
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
,
there exists an

n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
, effectively computable from the description of
\mathcal{A}
,
such that
\mathcal{A}
and
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
are equivalent.
In the case we start with
n
-
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
, the formulas we obtain are in
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, respectively.

This expressiveness equivalence will be useful in getting further decidability results, as well as separations in expressiveness,
for GNNs.

The idea of the proof of the theorem is that we have only finitely many elements in the overapproximation set to worry about, so we can fix each in turn and write a formula for each.
Our main translation is captured in the following:

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
n
-
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
,
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
and
\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,
there exists an
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
,
such that for every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(v)
if and only if

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}
.
In the case we start with
n
-
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
,
the formulas we obtain are in
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, respectively.

Proof 4.12
.

We define an
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
inductively on layers.
For the base case
\ell=0
,
for
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(0\right)}}
,
let
\theta_{i,1}(x):=U_{i}(x)
and
\theta_{i,0}(x):=\neg U_{i}(x)
.
For
\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(0\right)}}
,

\varphi^{\left(0\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)\ :=\ \bigwedge_{i\in{\left[{d^{\left(0\right)}}\right]}}\theta_{i,\mathbf{s}_{i}}(x).

For the inductive case
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
for
{p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}}\leq k\leq{p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}}
,
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
, and
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(x)\ :=\ \left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime}+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)_{i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}\\
\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}\end{subarray}}\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{i}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\epsilon_{x}(x,y)\land\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{s^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right]}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}\left(\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\right)_{i}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}(y)\right]}\ \circledast_{k}\ \dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}},

where
{\epsilon_{\text{out}}(x,y):=E(x,y)}
and
{\epsilon_{\text{in}}(x,y):=E(y,x)}
.
If
k=p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
, then
\circledast_{k}
is
\leq
;
if
k=p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
, then
\circledast_{k}
is
\geq
;
otherwise,
\circledast_{k}
is
=
.
Finally, for
\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,

\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)\ :=\ \bigvee_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}\left(\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(x)\land\bigwedge_{i\in{\left[{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right]}}\bigvee_{k\in\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{s}_{i})}\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(x)\right),

where
\mathcal{K}(s):=\left\{k\in{\left[p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\mathinner{,}\penalty p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}\right]}\middle|\ f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=s\right\}
.
Note that by Lemma
4.6
,
the size of
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
is finite.
Thus the disjunction in the construction is over a finite set.

We prove the correctness of the construction by induction on the layers.

For the base case
\ell=0
,
for every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
, vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
and
\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(0\right)}}
,
it is straightforward to check that

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(0\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(v)
if and only if

\xi^{\left(0\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}
.

For the induction step
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
for every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and

\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
,
we define
\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}:V\to\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\ :=\ \left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime}+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}\\
\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\epsilon_{x}(v,u)\land\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}(u)\right\}\right|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\cdot\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}(u)\right\}\right|.

By the semantics of Presburger quantifiers,
for
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,

\mathcal{G}\models\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(v)
if and only if

\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\ \circledast_{k}\ \dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}
.
Let
{V_{s^{\prime\prime}}:=\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}(u)\right\}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
for every vertex
u
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}(u)
if and only if
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}
.
Hence
V_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}=\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\right\}
, and

\left\{V_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}\right\}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}
form a partition of
V
.
Thus, we can rewrite
\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\ =

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime}+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}\\
\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{x}(v)\cap V_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\right)+\sum_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}}\!\!\!\!\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\sum_{u\in V\cap V_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}}}\mathbf{s}^{\prime\prime}\right)

\displaystyle\ =

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(\ell\right)}\mathbf{s}^{\prime}+\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)+\sum_{x\in\left\{\text{out},\text{in}\right\}}\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{x}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)\right)+\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)

By the definition of features,
we obtain that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(v)}(v)\right)
.

If
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(v)
,
then there exists
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
such that

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)
.
By the induction hypothesis,

\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
.
For
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}
, because

\mathcal{G}\models\bigvee_{\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{s}_{i})}\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{n,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(x)
,
there exists
{p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}}\leq k\leq{p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}}

such that
f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=\mathbf{s}_{i}

and

\mathcal{G}\models\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(v)
.
By the semantics of Presburger quantifiers,

\mathcal{G}\models\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(v)
implies that

\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\ \circledast_{k}\ \dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}
.
Recall that
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
is eventually constant with the left threshold
t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
and reight threshold
t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
and

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{i}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\right)
.

•

If
k=p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
, then
\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\leq t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}
.
Thus,
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{i}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\right)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=\mathbf{s}_{i}
.

•

If
k=p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
, then
\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\geq t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
.
Thus,
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{i}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}\right)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=\mathbf{s}_{i}
.

•

If
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}\leq k\leq p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}
,
then
\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}=\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,
which implies that

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{i}(v)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=\mathbf{s}_{i}
.

Therefore
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}
.

On the other hand,
suppose that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)=\mathbf{s}
.
Let us denote
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(v)
by
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
.
By definition, the features are captured by spectrum,
\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)
.
For
1\leq i\leq{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}
, let

k\ :=\ \begin{cases}p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}},&\text{if $\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\leq t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{left}}$}\\
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}},&\text{if $\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i}\geq t^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathsf{right}}$}\\
p^{\left(\ell\right)}\cdot\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)_{i},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

By the semantics of Presburger quantifiers,

\mathcal{G}\models\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(v)
.
Furthermore,

f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\dfrac{k}{p^{\left(\ell\right)}}\right)=f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)

By the property of
\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)
,

f^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{w}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}^{\prime}}(v)\right)=\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)
.
Therefore

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(v)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is local, then for
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}
is a zero matrix.
Thus, there are no
\top
guard atoms in
\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(x)
.
Hence the formula we obtained is in
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
.

Similarly, if
\mathcal{A}
is outging-only, then for
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{in}}
is a zero matrix.
Thus, there are no
E(y,x)
guard atoms in
\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{k,\mathbf{s}^{\prime},i}(x)
.
Hence the formula we obtained is in
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
.

We can now prove Theorem
4.10
.

Proof 4.13
.

Let
{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(L\right)}_{\geq 1/2}}:=\left\{\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(L\right)}}\middle|\ \mathbf{s}_{1}\geq 1/2\right\}
and

\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x):=\bigvee_{\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(L\right)}_{\geq 1/2}}}\varphi^{\left(L\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
,
where
\varphi^{\left(L\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(x)
is the formulas defined in Lemma
4.11
.

For every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
if
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(v)
,
then there exists
{\mathbf{s}\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(L\right)}_{\geq 1/2}}}
,
such that
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(L\right)}_{\mathbf{s}}(v)
.
By Lemma
4.11
,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}(v)=s
.
Hence
{\mathbf{s}_{1}=\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{1}(v)\geq 1/2}
, which implies that
\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.

On the other hand, if
\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,
by Lemma
4.11
,

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi^{\left(L\right)}_{\xi^{\left(L\right)}(v)}(v)
.
By definition of acceptness of GNNs,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{1}(v)\geq 1/2
,
which implies that
\xi^{\left(L\right)}(v)\in{\uparrow\!{\mathcal{S}}^{\left(L\right)}_{\geq 1/2}}
.
Therefore
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(v)
.

If
\mathcal{A}
is an
n
-
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
(
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
), the formulas defined in Lemma
4.11
are in
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
(
n
-
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
).
Hence
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
is also in
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
(
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
).

Recall that finite satisfiability of
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
is decidable by Corollary
3.14
. Combining this with Theorem
4.10
we get decidability of satisfiability for
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
:

Theorem 4.14
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is decidable.

Recall from Theorem
3.13
that finite satisfiability for the richer logic
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
, allowing unguarded unary quantification and containing
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, is also decidable.
Using this with Theorem
4.10
gives decidability of universal satisfiability for
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.

Theorem 4.15
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is decidable.

Proof 4.16
.

For every
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
, by Theorem
4.10
,
there exists a
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
such that
\mathcal{A}
and
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
are equivalent.
We claim that
\mathcal{A}
is universally satisfiable if and only if the
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
sentence
\varphi:=\forall x\ (x=x)\to\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
is finitely satisfiable.

Suppose that
\psi
is finitely satisfiable by the graph
\mathcal{G}
.
Then for every
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(v)
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
and
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
are equivalent,
\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.
Hence
\mathcal{A}
is universally satisfiable, with witness graph
\mathcal{G}
.
On the other hand, Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is universally satisfiable, with witness the finite graph
\mathcal{G}
.
By definition, for every
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{A}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
and
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(x)
are equivalent,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{\mathcal{A}}(v)
.
Hence
\varphi
is satisfiable by
\mathcal{G}
.
Note that the size of
\mathcal{G}
is finite, which implies that
\varphi
is finitely satisfiable.

The following converse to Theorem
4.10
shows that the logic is equally expressive as the GNN model:

Theorem 4.17
.

For every
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\psi(x)
,
there exists an
n
-
\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\psi}
,
such that
\psi(x)
and
\mathcal{A}_{\psi}
are equivalent.
If we start with
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas, we obtain
n
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
and
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
, respectively.

The idea of the proof is induction on the formula structure. For each subformula there will be an entry of a feature for the GNN which represents the subformula, in the sense that – for the second to last layer –
its value is
1
if the subformula holds, or
0
otherwise. We will have an entry for each subformula at every iteration, but as we progress to later layers of the GNN, more of these entries will be correct with respect to the corresponding subformula. In an inductive case for a Presburger quantifier that uses some coefficients
\lambda_{t}
, the corresponding matrix coefficient will be multiplying certain quantifies by
\lambda_{t}
.
Note that this translation is polynomial time, thus the size of the corresponding GNN is polynomial in the formula.

For every
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\psi(x)
, let
L
be the number of subformulas of
\psi(x)
and
\left\{\varphi_{\ell}(x)\right\}_{\ell\in{\left[L\right]}}
be an enumeration of subformulas of
\psi(x)
satisfying that

\varphi_{L}(x)
is
\psi(x)

and for each
\varphi_{i}(x)
and
\varphi_{j}(x)
, if
\varphi_{i}(x)
is a strict subformula of
\varphi_{j}(x)
, then
i<j
.

Definition 4.18
.

For a
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\Psi(x)
, for a graph
\mathcal{G}
and a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
we say that a vector
\mathbf{v}
is
\ell
-correct
for
\Psi(x)
w.r.t.
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,
if, for
1\leq t\leq\ell
,
\mathbf{v}_{t}={\left\llbracket\varphi_{t}(v)\right\rrbracket}_{\mathcal{G}}
.

We define the
(L+1)
-layer
n
-
\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
as follows.
The input dimension
{d^{\left(0\right)}}
is
n
.
For
1\leq\ell\leq L
, the dimension
{d^{\left(\ell\right)}}
is
L+n
, and
{d^{\left(L+1\right)}}=1
.
The coefficient matrices and bias vectors are chosen so that the features of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
satisfies the following conditions.

We first describe the conditions for the first
L
entries of features.
For
1\leq\ell\leq L
,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(L-1\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \cdots\ =\ \xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v).

The condition of
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)
depend on the formula
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\top
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=1
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
U_{j}(x)
for some unary predicate
U_{j}
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{L+j}(v)
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\neg\varphi_{j}(x)
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=\mathsf{TrReLU}\left(1-\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j}(v)\right)
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\varphi_{j_{1}}(x)\land\varphi_{j_{2}}(x)
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=\mathsf{TrReLU}\left(\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{1}}(v)+\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{2}}(v)-1\right)
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
{\left(\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\ \lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\land\varphi_{j_{t}}(y)\right]}\geq\delta\right)}
,
then

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{TrReLU}\left(\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot\sum_{u\in X_{t}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{t}}(u)-\delta+1\right),

where

X_{t}(v)\ :=\ \begin{cases}\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v),&\text{if $\epsilon_{t}(x,y)$ is $E(x,y)$}\\
\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(v),&\text{if $\epsilon_{t}(x,y)$ is $E(y,x)$}\\
V,&\text{if $\epsilon_{t}(x,y)$ is $\top$}.\end{cases}

Next, the conditions for the last
n
entries of features are as follows:
for
1\leq t\leq n
,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{L+t}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(L-1\right)}_{L+t}(v)\ =\ \cdots\ =\ \xi^{\left(1\right)}_{L+t}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(0\right)}_{t}(v).

Finally, for the last layer,
\xi^{\left(L+1\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{L}(v)
.

It is easy to see that there are GNNs that satisfy these conditions.

The theorem will follow once we have shown the following property of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
:

Lemma 4.19
.

For every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
for
1\leq\ell\leq L
, the feature

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)
within
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
is
\ell
-correct for
\Psi(x)
w.r.t.
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.

Proof 4.20
.

For
1\leq\ell\leq L
,
for
1\leq t\leq\ell-1
,
note that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{t}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{t}(v)
.
By the induction hypothesis,

\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(v)
is
(\ell-1)
-correct for
\Psi(x)
w.r.t.
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,
which implies that
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{t}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{t}(v)\right\rrbracket}
.
Thus, it holds that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{t}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{t}(v)\right\rrbracket}
.

It remains to show that
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{\ell}(v)\right\rrbracket}
.
We prove the property by induction on subformulas.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\top
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=1
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
.

•

If
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
U_{j}(x)
for some unary predicate
U_{j}
,
then
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{L+j}(v)
.
Note that
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{L+j}(v)=\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{j}(v)
.
If
\mathcal{G}\models U_{j}(v)
,
then
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{j}(v)=1
,
which implies
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=1
.
Otherwise, if
\mathcal{G}\not\models U_{j}(v)
,
then
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{j}(v)=0

which implies
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=0
.

•

Suppose that
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\neg\varphi_{j}(x)
.
Because
\varphi_{j}(x)
is a strict subformula of
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
,
\ell-1\geq{j}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{j}(v)\right\rrbracket}
.
Thus, we have

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{TrReLU}\left(1-{\left\llbracket\varphi_{j}(v)\right\rrbracket}\right)\ =\ {\left\llbracket\varphi_{\ell}(v)\right\rrbracket}.

•

Suppose that
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
\varphi_{j_{1}}(x)\land\varphi_{j_{2}}(x)
.
Because
\varphi_{j_{1}}(x)
and
\varphi_{j_{2}}(x)
are strict subformulas of
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
,

\ell-1\geq j_{1}
and
\ell-1\geq j_{2}
.
By the induction hypothesis,

\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{1}}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{1}}(v)\right\rrbracket}
and

\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{2}}(v)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{2}}(v)\right\rrbracket}
.
Thus, we have

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{TrReLU}\left({\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{1}}(v)\right\rrbracket}+{\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{2}}(v)\right\rrbracket}-1\right)\ =\ {\left\llbracket\varphi_{\ell}(v)\right\rrbracket}.

•

Suppose that
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
is
{\left(\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\ \lambda_{t}\cdot{\#_{y}\left[\epsilon_{t}(x,y)\land\varphi_{j_{t}}(y)\right]}\geq\delta\right)}
.
For
1\leq t\leq k
,
because
\varphi_{j_{t}}(x)
is a strict subformula of
\varphi_{\ell}(x)
,

\ell-1\geq{j_{t}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
for every
u
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{t}}(u)={\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{t}}(u)\right\rrbracket}
.
We note that

\displaystyle\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\epsilon_{t}(v,u)\land\varphi_{j_{t}}(u)\right\}\right|\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in X_{t}(v)}{\left\llbracket\varphi_{j_{t}}(u)\right\rrbracket}\ =\

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in X_{t}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{t}}(u).

Let
w(v)
be a value defined as follows:

w(v)\ :=\ \sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\epsilon_{t}(v,u)\land\varphi_{j_{t}}(u)\right\}\right|\ =\ \sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot\sum_{u\in X_{t}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{t}}(u).

Observe that

\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{TrReLU}\left(\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\lambda_{t}\cdot\sum_{u\in X_{t}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{j_{t}}(u)-\delta+1\right)\ =\ \mathsf{TrReLU}\left(w(v)-\delta+1\right).

By the semantic of Presburger quantifiers,
if
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{\ell}(v)
, then
w(v)\geq\delta
,
which implies
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=1
.
On the other hand,
if
\mathcal{G}\not\models\varphi_{\ell}(v)
, then
w(v)<\delta
,
which implies
\xi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\ell}(v)=0
.

We can now prove Theorem
4.17
.

Proof 4.21
.

For every
n
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\Psi(x)
,
let
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
be the
n
-
\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined in Lemma
4.19
.
Note that
\xi^{\left(L+1\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{L}(v)
.

For every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
if
\mathcal{G}\models\Psi(v)
,
since
\varphi_{L}(x)
is
\Psi(x)
,
we have
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{L}(v)
.
By Lemma
4.19
,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{L}(v)=1
,
which implies
\xi^{\left(L+1\right)}_{1}(v)=1
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.
On the other hand,
if
\mathcal{G}\not\models\Psi(v)
,
we have
\mathcal{G}\not\models\varphi_{L}(v)
.
By Lemma
4.19
again,

\xi^{\left(L\right)}_{L}(v)=0
,
which implies
\xi^{\left(L+1\right)}_{1}(v)=0
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
does not accept
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
.

If
\Psi(x)
is an
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula,
by construction,
for
1\leq\ell\leq L
,

\mathbf{R}^{\left(\ell\right)}
is a zero matrix.
Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
is an
n
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.
Similarly, if
\Psi(x)
is an
n
-
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula,
by construction,
for
1\leq\ell\leq L
,

\mathbf{A}^{\left(\ell\right)}_{\text{in}}
is a zero matrix.
Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
is an
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.

Putting together the two translation results, we have:

Corollary 4.22
.

The logic
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s are expressively equivalent,
as are
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s;
\mathcal{O}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
and
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.

The translations also tell us that
the expressiveness of GNNs with
\mathsf{TrReLU}

is the same as that of GNNs with arbitrary eventually constant activations
– provided we use the Boolean semantics based on thresholds.

4.2
Undecidability of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, and of GNNs with
\mathsf{TrReLU}
and global readout

Note that we claimed in Theorem
4.2
that the spectrum is finite for GNNs with eventually constant activations, even when they have global readout. And we could compute a finite overapproximation of the spectrum. But in our decidability argument for
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
, we required further the ability to decide membership in the spectrum for any fixed rational, and for this we utilized decidability of the logic. So what happens to decidability of the GNNs – or the corresponding logic – when global readout is allowed?

We show undecidability of finite satisfiability for the logic
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
, and of the corresponding GNN satisfiability problem. First for the logic:

Theorem 4.23
.

The finite satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
is undecidable.

For the proof we apply an approach based on ideas
in
[
5
]
,
using a reduction from Hilbert’s tenth problem.

Definition 4.24
.

A
simple equation system
\varepsilon
(with
n
variables and
m
equations) is a set of
m

equations of one of the forms
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, or
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
where
1\leq t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}\leq n
are pairwise distinct.
We say the system
\varepsilon
is solvable if it has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
.

Lemma 4.25
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
there exists an
(n+m)
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)

such that the following are equivalent.

1.

The system
\varepsilon
has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
.

2.

There exists an
(n+m)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
such that for every vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{G}\models\Psi_{\varepsilon}(v)
.

3.

The formula
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
is finitely satisfiable.

Proof 4.26
.

We construct the formula
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
as follows.
The vocabulary of
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
consists of unary predicates
\left\{P_{t}\right\}_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}
and
\left\{U_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
.
For
1\leq t\leq m
, we define
\varphi_{t}(x)
depending on the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\varphi_{t}(x):=({\#_{y}\left[P_{t}(y)\land U_{t_{1}}(y)\right]}=1)
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\varphi_{t}(x):=({\#_{y}\left[\psi_{t}(y)\right]}={\#_{y}\left[\top\right]})
where

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(y)\ :=

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}(y)\land(U_{t_{2}}(y)\lor U_{t_{3}}(y))\to\left({\#_{x}\left[E(y,x)\land P_{t}(x)\land U_{t_{1}}(x)\right]}=1\right)\right)\ \land\

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}(y)\land U_{t_{1}}(y)\to\left({\#_{x}\left[E(x,y)\land P_{t}(x)\land(U_{t_{2}}(x)\lor U_{t_{3}}(x))\right]}=1\right)\right).

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\varphi_{t}(x):=({\#_{y}\left[\psi_{t}(y)\right]}={\#_{y}\left[\top\right]})
where

\displaystyle\psi_{t}(y)\ :=

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}(y)\land U_{t_{2}}(y)\to\left({\#_{x}\left[E(y,x)\land P_{t}(x)\land U_{t_{1}}(x)\right]}={\#_{x}\left[P_{t}(x)\land U_{t_{3}}(x)\right]}\right)\right)\ \land\

\displaystyle\left(P_{t}(y)\land U_{t_{1}}(y)\to\left({\#_{x}\left[E(x,y)\land P_{t}(x)\land U_{t_{2}}(x)\right]}=1\right)\right).

We now define
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
:

\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)\ :=\ \psi^{\mathsf{disj}}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{eq}}(x)\land\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\varphi_{t}(x),

where

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{disj}}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}t,t^{\prime}\in{\left[m\right]}\\
t\neq t^{\prime}\end{subarray}}({\#_{y}\left[P_{t}(y)\land P_{t^{\prime}}(y)\right]}=0)\ \land\ \bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,i^{\prime}\in{\left[n\right]}\\
i\neq i^{\prime}\end{subarray}}({\#_{y}\left[U_{i}(y)\land U_{i^{\prime}}(y)\right]}=0)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{eq}}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}t,t^{\prime}\in{\left[m\right]}\\
i\in{\left[n\right]}\end{subarray}}({\#_{y}\left[P_{t}(y)\land U_{i}(y)\right]}={\#_{y}\left[P_{t^{\prime}}(y)\land U_{i}(y)\right]})

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
, suppose
\varepsilon
has a solution

\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be the
(n+m)
-graph defined as follows.
For
1\leq t\leq m
,
1\leq i\leq n
, and
1\leq j\leq a_{i}
, let
v_{t,i,j}
be a fresh vertex
and
V
be the set of all such vertices.
For
1\leq t\leq m
,
let
P_{t}:=\left\{v_{t,i,j}\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq n;1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
.
For
1\leq i\leq n
,
let
U_{i}:=\left\{v_{t,i,j}\middle|\ 1\leq t\leq m;1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
.
For
1\leq t\leq m
, we define

E_{t}
depending on the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
E_{t}:=\emptyset
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

E_{t}:=\left\{(v_{t,t_{2},j},v_{t,t_{1},j})\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{2}}\right\}\cup\left\{(v_{t,t_{3},j},v_{t,t_{1},a_{t_{2}}+j})\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}}\right\}.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

E_{t}:=\left\{(v_{t,t_{2},j},v_{t,t_{1},(j-1)\cdot a_{t_{3}}+j^{\prime}})\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{2}};1\leq j^{\prime}\leq a_{t_{3}}\right\}.

Finally, let
E:=\ \bigcup_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}E_{t}
.

Note that
\left\{P_{t}\right\}_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}

and
\left\{U_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
form partitions of
V
, respectively.
Furthermore, for
1\leq t\leq m
and
1\leq i\leq n
,

\left|P_{t}\cap U_{i}\right|=a_{i}
.
Thus, it is straightforward to verify that for every
v\in V
,
it holds that
\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{eq}}(v)\land\psi^{\mathsf{disj}}(v)
.
For
1\leq t\leq m
, we check the satisfiability of
\varphi_{t}(x)
depending on the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
a_{t_{1}}=1
.
Thus
\left|P_{t}\cap U_{t_{1}}\right|=1
,
which implis that for every
v\in V
,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{t}(v)
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
a_{t_{1}}=a_{t_{2}}+a_{t_{3}}
.
We first claim that for every
u\in V
,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)
.

–

If
u=v_{t,t_{2},j}
for some
1\leq j\leq a_{t_{2}}
,
then
\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ E(u,u^{\prime})\land P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land U_{t_{1}}(u^{\prime})\right\}\right|=\left|\left\{v_{t,t_{1},j}\right\}\right|=1
.

–

If
u=v_{t,t_{3},j}
for some
1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}}
,
then
\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ E(u,u^{\prime})\land P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land U_{t_{1}}(u^{\prime})\right\}\right|=\left|\left\{v_{t,t_{1},a_{t_{2}}+j}\right\}\right|=1
.

–

If
u=v_{t,t_{1},j}
for some
1\leq j\leq a_{t_{1}}
,
if
j\leq a_{t_{2}}
, then there is only one edge from
v_{t,t_{2},j}
to
u
.
Otherwise, there is only one edge from
v_{t,t_{3},j-a_{t_{2}}}
to
u
.
Thus
\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ E(u^{\prime},u)\land P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land\left(U_{t_{2}}(u^{\prime})\lor U_{t_{3}}(u^{\prime})\right)\right\}\right|=1
.

Therefore, for every
v\in V
,
since
\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)\right\}\right|=\left|V\right|
,
it holds that
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{t}(v)
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
a_{t_{1}}=a_{t_{2}}\cdot a_{t_{3}}
.
We first claim that for every
u\in V
,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)
.

–

If
u=v_{t,t_{2},j}
for some
1\leq j\leq a_{t_{2}}
,
then

\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ E(u,u^{\prime})\land P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land U_{t_{1}}(u^{\prime})\right\}\right|\ =\ \left|\left\{v_{i,t_{1},(j-1)\cdot a_{t_{3}}+j^{\prime}}\middle|\ 1\leq j^{\prime}\leq a_{t_{3}}\right\}\right|\ =\ a_{t_{3}}.

Note that

\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land U_{t_{3}}(u^{\prime})\right\}\right|=\left|P_{t}\cap U_{t_{3}}\right|=a_{t_{3}}
.

–

If
u=v_{t,t_{1},k}
for some
1\leq j\leq a_{t_{1}}
,
then there is only one edge from
v_{t,t_{2},j^{\prime}}
to
u
,
where
j^{\prime}=\left\lfloor j/a_{t_{2}}\right\rfloor+1
.
Thus
\left|\left\{u^{\prime}\in V\middle|\ E(u^{\prime},u)\land P_{t}(u^{\prime})\land U_{t_{2}}(u^{\prime})\right\}\right|=1
.

Therefore, for every
v\in V
,
since
\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)\right\}\right|=\left|V\right|
,

\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{t}(v)
.

Hence for every vertex
v\in V
,
\mathcal{G}\models\Psi_{\varepsilon}(v)
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3)
,
it is clear that (3) is a simple corollary (2).

(3)
\Rightarrow
(1)
,
suppose
\mathcal{G}\models\Psi_{\varepsilon}(v)
.
First of all,
for
1\leq t\leq m
and
1\leq i\leq n
,
let

V_{t,i}\ :=\ \left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models P_{t}(u)\land U_{i}(u)\right\}.

Because
{\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{disj}}(v)}
,

V_{t,i}
forms a partition of
V
.
Next, because
\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{eq}}(v)
,
for every
1\leq t<t^{\prime}\leq m
, and
1\leq i\leq n
,

\left|V_{t_{1},i}\right|=\left|V_{t_{2},i}\right|
.
Finally, we claim that

\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow\left|V_{1,i}\right|\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}

is a solution of
\varepsilon
.
We show that
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{t}(v)
implies that

\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow\left|V_{1,i}\right|\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
satisfies the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\left({\#_{y}\left[P_{t}(y)\land U_{t_{1}}(y)\right]}=1\right)
,
which implies that

\left|V_{t,t_{1}}\right|=1
.
Hence
\left|V_{1,t_{1}}\right|=\left|V_{t,t_{1}}\right|=1
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\left({\#_{y}\left[\psi_{t}(y)\right]}={\#_{y}\left[\top\right]}\right)
, which implies that

\left|\left\{u\in V\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)\right\}\right|=\left|V\right|
.
Hence for each
u\in V
,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)
.

We will argue that the edges between
V_{t,t_{2}}\cup V_{t,t_{3}}
and
V_{t,t_{1}}
give us a bijection, which will establish the satisfaction of the equation.
For each
u\in V_{t,t_{2}}\cup V_{t,t_{3}}
,
since
\mathcal{G}\models\left({\#_{x}\left[E(u,x)\land P_{t}(x)\land U_{t_{1}}(x)\right]}=1\right)
,
there exists only one edge from
u
to the set
V_{t,t_{1}}
.
On the other hand,
for each
u\in V_{t,t_{1}}
,

\mathcal{G}\models\left({\#_{x}\left[E(x,u)\land P(x)\land(U_{t_{2}}(x)\lor U_{t_{3}}(x))\right]}=1\right)
,
which implies that there exists only one edge from the set
V_{t,t_{2}}\cup V_{t,t_{3}}
to
u
.
Therefore,
by considering the number of edges from the set
V_{t,t_{2}}\cup V_{t,t_{3}}
to
V_{t,t_{1}}
,
we have

1\cdot\left|V_{t,t_{1}}\right|=1\cdot\left|V_{t,t_{2}}\cup V_{t,t_{3}}\right|
.
Thus
\left|V_{t,t_{1}}\right|=\left|V_{t,t_{2}}\right|+\left|V_{t,t_{3}}\right|
,
since
V_{t,t_{2}}
and
V_{t,t_{3}}
are disjoint.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
by an argument similar to the one above,
for each
u\in V
,
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{t}(u)
.

We will establish the equality by using the edges from the set
V_{t,t_{2}}
to the set
V_{t,t_{1}}

to show that there is a
\left|V_{t,t_{3}}\right|
-to-one relationship from
V_{t,t_{2}}
to
