\xi^{\left(\ell_{0}+2h_{i}\right)}_{n+i}(v)=0
.

We begin with the encoding for Theorem
6.12
.
We demonstrate the ability of
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s to check multiplication by the following example.

The idea will be that we construct a
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
such that the sizes of nodes satisfying certain unary predicates, within models of
\mathcal{A}
, will correspond to solutions of a set of equations.

For example, consider simulating a single equation
x\cdot y=a
.
for
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{a}
be the
3
-layer 1-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

defined as follows.
The dimensions are
d_{0}=d_{1}=d_{3}=1
and
d_{2}=2
.
The coefficient matrices and bias vectors will be chosen so that the features satisfy the following conditions,

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{1}(u)\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u)-a\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{2}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(a-\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u)\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(1-\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(v)-\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{2}(v)\right).

We claim that
\mathcal{A}_{a}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
if and only if
a=\left|U_{1}\right|\cdot\left|V\right|
, where
U_{1}
is the unique input predicate.
To see this, note that
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(v)=\left|U_{1}\right|
and
\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u)=\left|U_{1}\right|\cdot\left|V\right|
.
For the last layer,
\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{1}(v)=1
if and only if
\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{2}(v)=0
,
that is,
\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u)=a
.

We now turn from intuition to the details of the construction.

Lemma 6.16
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
there exists an
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}

such that

\varepsilon
has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable.

Proof 6.17
.

We define the 15-layer
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
with
integer coefficient matrices and bias vectors as follows.
The dimensions, coefficient matrices, and bias vectors will be chosen so that the features of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy a set of conditions, which we now describe.
Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ \land\ \left(x_{n+2m+1}=x_{n+2m+2}\right),

where, for
1\leq t\leq m
,

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and

\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=1\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and

\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=x_{t_{2}}+x_{t_{3}}\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then

\displaystyle\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(\left(x_{n+t}=0\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=0\right)\right)\lor\left(\left(x_{n+t}=x_{t_{2}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+t}=x_{t_{1}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=x_{t_{3}}\right).

For the first layer,

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $n<i\leq n+m$}\\
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $n+m<i\leq n+2m$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=n+2m+1$}.\end{cases}

Foe the second to the ninth layers,
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test
whether
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m+1$}\\
{\left\llbracket\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

For the tenth layer,

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $n<i\leq n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

Foe the eleventh to the fourteenth layers,
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test
whether
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m+2$}\\
{\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+2m+3$}.\end{cases}

Finally, for the last layer,
\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)
.
By Lemma
6.14
, it is easy to see that there are
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s that satisfy these conditions.

We now show that this construction has the required properties.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable
by the graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
a_{i}:=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)
.
We will show that
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
is a solution of
\varepsilon
.
Note that for
1\leq i\leq n
and
u\in V
,
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(u)=a_{i}
.

We first make a claim.
Fix
1\leq t\leq m
,
and suppose that the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
.
Then for every
u\in V
,

\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

We now prove the claim.
Because
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfied by
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)={\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket}=1
.
Since
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)
holds,
it follows that

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+2m+1}(v)=\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+2m+2}(v).

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
,
we can rewrite both sides as follows:

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+2m+1}(v)\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+2m+1}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+2m+1}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}1

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+2m+2}(v)\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+2m+2}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}{\left\llbracket\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u)\right)\right\rrbracket},

which implies that, for every
u\in V
,
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u)\right)
holds.

Fix
1\leq t\leq m
,
and suppose that the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
.
Since
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u)\right)
holds,

\Psi_{1,t}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u)\right)
also holds.
Thus, one of the following must hold:

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(u)=0\land\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=0\quad\text{or}\quad\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(u)=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{t_{2}}(u)\land\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=1.

Note that
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{t_{2}}(u)=a_{t_{2}}
, and, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
,

\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ \xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)\ =\ \xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

Therefore, for both cases, it holds that
\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(u)=a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)
.

We now show that
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
is a solution of
\varepsilon
.
Because
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfied by
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)={\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket}=1
.
For
1\leq t\leq m
,
since
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)
holds,

\Psi_{2,t}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)
also holds.
We consider the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
.
Note that, for
1\leq i\leq n
,
\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v)=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)=a_{i}
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{1}}(v)=1
,
which implies that

a_{t_{1}}=1
.

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{1}}(v)=\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{2}}(v)+\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{3}}(v)
,
which implies that

a_{t_{1}}=a_{t_{2}}+a_{t_{3}}
,

•

If the
t^{th}
equation is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{1}}(v)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(10\right)}_{t_{3}}(v).

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(v)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(v)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

Combining with the claim above,
it holds that

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(v)=a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(v)
,
which implies that

a_{t_{1}}=a_{t_{2}}\cdot a_{t_{3}}
.

Suppose
\varepsilon
is solvable
.
Let
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
be a solution of
\varepsilon
and

\tilde{a}
be the maximum value of
a_{i}
.
Note that in Definition
4.24
, we define a system to be solvable if it has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
, thus the solution consists of natural numbers.
We construct the following
(n+2m)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
depending on the solution of
\varepsilon
.
For
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq\tilde{a}
,
let
v_{j}
and
v_{j,j^{\prime}}
be fresh vertices and
V
be the set of all such vertices.
The colors of
\mathcal{G}
are defined as follows.

•

For
1\leq i\leq n
,
U_{i}:=\left\{v_{j}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
.

•

For
1\leq t\leq m
,
if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is

\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

U_{n+t}:=\left\{v_{j,j^{\prime}}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}},1\leq j^{\prime}\leq a_{t_{2}}\right\}
and

U_{n+m+t}:=\left\{v_{j}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}}\right\}
.
Otherwise,

U_{n+t}:=\emptyset
and
U_{n+m+t}:=\emptyset
.

Finally, the edges of
\mathcal{G}
are
\left\{(v_{j},v_{j,j^{\prime}})\middle|\ 1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq\tilde{a}\right\}
.

We now claim that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v_{1}\right\rangle
.
We start from computing the first feature of vertices.

•

For
1\leq j\leq\tilde{a}
,
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v_{j})=a_{i}
.
For
1\leq t\leq m
,
if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is

\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
and
j\leq a_{t_{3}}
,
then
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(v_{j})=a_{t_{2}}
and
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(v_{j})=1
.
Otherwise,
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(v_{j})=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(v_{j})=0
.

•

For
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq\tilde{a}
,
for
1\leq t\leq m
,
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+t}(v_{j,j^{\prime}})=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+m+t}(v_{j,j^{\prime}})=0
.

Then, it is clear that, for every vertex
u\in V
,

\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(u)={\left\llbracket\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u)\right)\right\rrbracket}=1
.

Next, we compute the tenth feature of
v_{1}
.
For
1\leq i\leq n
,

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v_{1})=\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(v_{1})=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v_{1})=a_{i}.

For
1\leq t\leq n
,
suppose that the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is

\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(v_{1})\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ \sum_{j\in{\left[a_{t_{3}}\right]}}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+t}(v_{j})\ =\ \sum_{j\in{\left[a_{t_{3}}\right]}}a_{t_{2}}\ =\ a_{t_{2}}\cdot a_{t_{3}}

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(v_{1})\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)\ =\ \sum_{j\in{\left[a_{t_{3}}\right]}}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{n+m+t}(v_{j})\ =\ \sum_{j\in{\left[a_{t_{3}}\right]}}1\ =\ a_{t_{3}}

Finally, it is straightforward to check that

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v_{1})=\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v_{1})={\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v_{1})\right)\right\rrbracket}=1
,
which implies that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v_{1}\right\rangle
.

This completes the proof of Lemma
6.16
, and
thus the proof of Theorem
6.12
.

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
6.13
.
The idea is similar to Theorem
6.12
.
However, we now encode the solution of a set of equations in the number of children satisfying certain unary predicates.

For example, consider simulating a single equation
x\cdot y=a
.
for
a\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{a}
be the
4
-layer 1-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

defined as follows.
The dimensions are
d_{0}=d_{1}=d_{2}=d_{4}=1
and
d_{3}=2
.
The coefficient matrices and bias vectors will be chosen so that the features satisfy the following conditions,

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{1}(u)\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(v)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u)\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(u)-a\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{2}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(a-\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(u)\right),

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(4\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \mathsf{ReLU}\left(1-\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{1}(v)-\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{2}(v)\right).

We claim that, for every
1
-level tree
\mathcal{T}
with root vertex
v_{r}
,
\mathcal{A}_{a}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{T},v_{r}\right\rangle
if and only if
a=\left|\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|\cdot\left|U_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|
, where
U_{1}
is the unique input predicate.
To see this, note that
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(v)=\left|U_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|
, and,
for every
u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})
,
\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)=\left\{v_{r}\right\}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(u)\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\left|U_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|

\displaystyle\ =

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|\cdot\left|U_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})\right|

For the last layer,
\xi^{\left(4\right)}_{1}(v_{r})=1
if and only if
\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{1}(v_{r})=\xi^{\left(3\right)}_{2}(v_{r})=0
,
that is,
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{1}(u)=a
.

The correctness of the above example is based on the fact that the children of the root vertex in the
1
-level tree have only one incoming neighbor – in this case, the root vertex.
Using separate incoming and outgoing aggregations, we can enforce the “single incoming neighbor constraint” on an arbitrary vertex, which will allow us encode iterative multiplication.

The general reduction is given in the following lemma:

Lemma 6.18
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
there exists an
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}

such that

\varepsilon
has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable.

Proof 6.19
.

We define the 16-layer
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
with
integer coefficient matrices and bias vectors as follows.
The dimensions, coefficient matrices, and bias vectors will be chosen so that the features of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy certain conditions, to be described next.
Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})\ \land\ \left(x_{2n+2m+1}=1\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ \land\ \left(x_{n+2m+1}=x_{n+2m+2}\right),

where, for
1\leq t\leq m
,

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and
\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=1\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and

\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=x_{t_{2}}+x_{t_{3}}\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then

\displaystyle\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(\left(x_{n+t}=0\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=0\right)\right)\lor\left(\left(x_{n+t}=x_{n+2m+t_{2}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+t}=x_{t_{1}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=x_{t_{3}}\right).

For the first layer,

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+m$}\\
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $n+m<i\leq n+2m$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=n+2m+1$}.\end{cases}

For the second layer,

\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(v)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i-n-2m}(u),&\text{for $n+2m<i\leq 2n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(v)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i-n}(u),&\text{for $i=2n+2m+1$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=2n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

For the third to the tenth layers,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test whether
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq 2n+2m+2$}\\
{\left\llbracket\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=2n+2m+3$}.\end{cases}

For the eleventh layer,

\xi^{\left(11\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}}\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $n<i\leq n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}}\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i+n+1}(u),&\text{for $n+2m<i\leq n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

For the twelfth to the fifteeneth layers,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test whether
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(11\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(11\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m+2$}\\
{\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(11\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+2m+3$}.\end{cases}

Finally, for the last layer,
\xi^{\left(16\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)
.
By Lemma
6.14
, it is easy to see that there are
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s that satisfy these conditions.

We now show that this construction has the required properties for the lemma.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable
by the graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
a_{i}:=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)
.
We will show that
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
is a solution of
\varepsilon
.

The proof idea is similar to Lemma
6.16
,
with the only difference being the following variant of the main claim used in that lemma.
For every
u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)
, both properties hold.

•

\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)=\left\{v\right\}
.

•

For
1\leq t\leq m
,
if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

We now prove the claim.
By the argument similar to Lemma
6.16
.
For every
u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)
,

\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(u)\right)
holds.

•

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{2n+2m+1}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{n+2m+1}(u^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)}1\ =\ \left|\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)\right|.

Because
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(u)\right)
holds, it follows that
\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{2n+2m+1}(u)=1
,
which implies that
u
has exactly one incoming neighbor.
Since
u
is a outgoing neighbor of
v
,
v
is an incoming neighbor of
u
.
Thus, it holds that
\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)=\left\{v\right\}
.

•

Fix
1\leq t\leq n
, and suppose that
the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
.
Since
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(u)\right)
holds,

\Psi_{1,t}\left(\xi^{\left(2\right)}(u)\right)
also holds.
Thus, one of the following must hold:

\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+t}(u)=0\land\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=0\quad\text{or}\quad\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+t}(u)=\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+2m+t_{2}}(u)\land\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=1.

Note that by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ \xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+t}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)\ =\ \xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

Combining with the previous claim,
\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)=\left\{v\right\}
,
it follows that

\xi^{\left(2\right)}_{n+2m+t_{2}}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{in}}(u)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{t_{2}}(u^{\prime})\ =\ \xi^{\left(1\right)}_{t_{2}}(v)\ =\ a_{t_{2}}.

Therefore, for both cases, it holds that

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+t}(u)=a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)
.

Suppose
\varepsilon
is solvable
.
Let
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
be a solution of
\varepsilon
and

\tilde{a}
be the maximum value of
a_{i}
.
We construct the following
(n+2m)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
depending on the solution of
\varepsilon
.
For
0\leq j\leq\tilde{a}
and
1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\tilde{a}
,
let
v_{j}
and
v_{j,j^{\prime}}
be fresh vertices and
V
be the set of all such vertices.
The colors of
\mathcal{G}
are defined as follows.

•

For
1\leq i\leq n
,
U_{i}=\left\{v_{0,j}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
.

•

For
1\leq t\leq m
,
if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is

\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
, then

U_{n+t}:=\left\{v_{j,j^{\prime}}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}},1\leq j^{\prime}\leq a_{t_{2}}\right\}
and

U_{n+m+t}:=\left\{v_{j}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{t_{3}}\right\}
.
Otherwise,

U_{n+t}:=\emptyset
and
U_{n+m+t}:=\emptyset
.

Finally, the edges of
\mathcal{G}
are

E\ :=\ \left\{(v_{j},v_{j,j^{\prime}})\middle|\ 0\leq j\leq\tilde{a},1\leq j^{\prime}\leq\tilde{a}\right\}\ \cup\ \left\{(v_{0},v_{j})\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq\tilde{a}\right\}.

We can show that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v_{0}\right\rangle

via an argument similar to the one used in Lemma
6.16
.

Since the solvability (over
\mathbb{N}
) of simple equation systems is undecidable,
we obtain undecidability of the satisfiability problems for
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

that is, Theorem
6.13
.

6.3
Undecidability of the universal satisfiability problem for GNNs with unbounded activations

Next, we turn to the universal satisfiability problem for GNNs with
\mathsf{ReLU}
activations.
We first show that the universal satisfiability for
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is undecidable.
The proof is also by reducing Hilbert’s tenth problem to it.

Lemma 6.20
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
let
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
be the
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined in Lemma
6.16
.
For every
(n+2m)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertices
v,v^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle

if and only if it accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v^{\prime}\right\rangle
.

Proof 6.21
.

We prove the lemma by analyzing the dependency of features across layers.
For every
v\in V
,
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
in Lemma
6.16
,
it holds that

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)\ =\ {\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(10\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket}.

For
1\leq i\leq n
,

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),

and for
n<i\leq n+2m+2
,

\xi^{\left(10\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \sum_{u\in V}\xi^{\left(9\right)}_{i}(v).

Observe that the value of
\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)
is independent of the neighbors of
v
.
Therefore, for every pair of vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in
,
\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{1}(v^{\prime})
,
which implies that

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle

if and only if it accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v^{\prime}\right\rangle
.

Note that it is obvious that if
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is universal satisfiable,
then it is satisfiable.
Therefore, by Lemma
6.16
,
the simple equation system
\varepsilon
has a solution in
\mathbb{N}

if and only if
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is universally satisfiable.
Thus we have the following undecidability result.

Theorem 6.22
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is undecidable.

For
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s,
note that we do not claim an expressive equivalence with a logic in Section
6.1
.
Nevertheless this containment of the logic
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
in the GNN class is useful, since
we can show undecidability of the logic
by reduction from the halting problem of two-counter machines,
which is known to be
undecidable
[
17
]
.

Definition 6.23
.

A
two-counter machine

\mathcal{M}
is a finite list of
n
instructions having one of the forms
\texttt{INC}\ (c_{i})
,
\texttt{IF}\ (c_{i}=0)\ \texttt{GOTO}\ ({q})
, or
HALT
,
where
i\in\left\{0,1\right\}
and
1\leq q\leq n
.

A
configuration
is a tuple
\left\langle q,c_{0},c_{1}\right\rangle
, where
1\leq q\leq n
and
c_{0},c_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
.
We say
\left\langle q^{\prime},c^{\prime}_{0},c^{\prime}_{1}\right\rangle
is the
successor configuration
of
\left\langle q,c_{0},c_{1}\right\rangle

depending on the
q^{th}
instruction of the machine.

•

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{INC}\ (c_{i})
,
then
q^{\prime}=q+1
,
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}+1
, and
c^{\prime}_{1-i}=c_{1-i}
.

•

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{IF}\ (c_{i}=0)\ \texttt{GOTO}\ ({j})
,
if
c_{i}=0
, then
q^{\prime}=j
,
c^{\prime}_{0}=c_{0}
, and
c^{\prime}_{1}=c_{1}
;
otherwise,
q^{\prime}=q+1
,
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}-1
, and
c^{\prime}_{1-i}=c_{1-i}
.

Note that if the
q^{th}
instruction is
HALT
,
there is no successor.
This configuration is called a halting configuration.

The
computation
of the machine is a (possibly infinite) sequence of configurations where the first is
\left\langle 1,0,0\right\rangle
,
consecutive pairs are in the succcessor relationship above,
the last configuration is a halting configuration.
The machine
halts
if its computation is a finite sequence.

Lemma 6.24
.

For every two-counter machine
\mathcal{M}
with
n
instructions, there exists an
(n+5)
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
formula
\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
such that
\mathcal{M}
halts if and only if the formula
\forall x\ \Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
is finitely satisfiable.

S
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
Q_{8}
\cdots
Q_{7}
T
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{0}

Figure 3:
An example of the encoding of the computation of the two-counter machines to directed graphs.

The reduction is by encoding the computation of a two-counter machine into the graph directly.
We have illustrated it in Fig.
3
.
Each vertex is labeled with exactly one of
S,T,C_{0},C_{1},Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{n}
.
Informally,
S
and
T
will be indicators for the beginning and the end of the computation.
Each
Q_{i}
will represent the particular state of the configuration.
The value of the counters
i
is encoded by the number of outgoing neighbors with label
C_{i}
.

Proof 6.25
.

Let
\tau
be the vocabulary consisting of unary predicates

\left\{S,T,C_{0},C_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{Q_{q}\right\}_{q\in{\left[n\right]}}

and a binary predicate
E
.

We first define some useful gadgets.

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,\delta}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{z,y}\left[E(x,z)\land E(z,y)\land C_{i}(y)\right]}-{\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land C_{i}(y)\right]}=\delta\right)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{i}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land C_{i}(y)\right]}=0\right)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{j}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land Q_{j}(y)\right]}=1\right)

\displaystyle Q(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{q\in{\left[n\right]}}Q_{q}(x)

\displaystyle C(x)\ :=

\displaystyle C_{1}(x)\lor C_{2}(x)

Next, for
1\leq q\leq n
, we define the
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
formula
\psi_{q}(x)
depending on the
q^{th}
instruction of
\mathcal{M}
.

•

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{INC}\ (c_{i})
,
then
\psi_{q}(x):=\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{q+1}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,1}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1-i,0}(x)
.

•

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{IF}\ (c_{i}=0)\ \texttt{GOTO}\ ({j})
,
then

\displaystyle\psi_{q}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left(\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{i}(x)\to\psi^{\mathsf{if}}_{q}(x)\right)\land\left(\neg\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{i}(x)\to\psi^{\mathsf{else}}_{q}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{if}}_{q}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{j}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{0,0}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1,0}(x)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{else}}_{q}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{q+1}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,-1}(x)\land\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1-i,0}(x).

•

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
HALT
,
then
\psi_{q}(x):=\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land T(y)\right]}=1\right)
.

Finally, we define
\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
as follows.

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left(\bigvee_{U\in\left\{S,T,C_{0},C_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{Q_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}}U(x)\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}U_{1},U_{2}\in\left\{S,T,C_{0},C_{1}\right\}\cup\left\{Q_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}\\
U_{1}\neq U_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\neg U_{1}(x)\lor\neg U_{2}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\top\right]}=1\right)\land\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land Q_{1}(y)\land\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{0}(y)\land\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{1}(y)\right]}=1\right)

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land S(y)\right]}=1\right)\land\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\left(T(y)\lor C(y)\lor Q(y)\right)\right]}=0\right)

\displaystyle\varphi_{4}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land(Q(y)\lor T(y))\right]}=1\right)\land\left({\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land S(y)\right]}=1\right)\land

\displaystyle\left({\#_{y}\left[E(y,x)\land\left(S(y)\lor Q(y)\right)\right]}=1\right)\land\left({\#_{y}\left[E(y,x)\land\left(T(y)\lor C(y)\right)\right]}=0\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)\ :=

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(x)\land\left(S(x)\to\varphi_{2}(x)\right)\land\left(\left(C(x)\lor T(x)\right)\to\varphi_{3}(x)\right)\land\left(Q(x)\to\varphi_{4}(x)\right)\land

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{q\in{\left[n\right]}}\left(Q_{q}(x)\to\psi_{q}(x)\right)

If
\forall x\ \Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
is satisfied by the finite graph
\mathcal{G}
,
then we will argue that
\mathcal{M}
halts.
For every vertex
v\in V
,
because
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{1}(v)
,
v
is in exactly one of
S
,
T
,
C_{0}
,
C_{1}
, or
Q_{q}
for
1\leq q\leq n
.
We say that
v
is an
S
vertex if it is in
S
, and

u
is an
S
outgoing neighbor of
v
if
u
is an
S
vertex and
u
is an outgoing neighbor of
v
.
We use a similar naming convention in the other cases.

Recall that a configuration of
\mathcal{M}
is a tuple
\left\langle q,c_{0},c_{1}\right\rangle
, where
1\leq q\leq n
and
c_{0},c_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
.
For a
Q_{q}
vertex
v
, where
1\leq q\leq n
,
the configuration realized by
v
, denoted by
\mathsf{conf}(v)
, is defined as
\left\langle q,c_{0},c_{1}\right\rangle
,
where
c_{0}=\left|\left\{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models C_{0}(u)\right\}\right|
and

c_{1}=\left|\left\{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)\middle|\ \mathcal{G}\models C_{1}(u)\right\}\right|
.
We first make a claim about the number of incoming and outgoing neighbors of
v
, giving conditions on this number which will depend on the type of
v
.

•

If
v
is an
S
vertex,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{2}(v)
,
which implies that
v
has only one outgoing neighbor
u
, where
u
is a
Q
vertex.
Moreover, the configuration of
u
is
\left\langle 1,0,0\right\rangle
.

•

If
v
is a
T
,
C_{1}
, or
C_{2}
vertex,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{3}(v)
,
which implies that
v
has only one outgoing neighbor
u
, where
u
is an
S
vertex.

•

If
v
is a
Q_{q}
vertex, for
1\leq q\leq n
,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\varphi_{4}(v)
,
which implies that
v
has exactly one
Q
or
T
outgoing neighbor,
and exactly one
S
outgoing neighbor.
Additionally,
v
has only one incoming neighbor
u
, where
u
is either a
Q
or
S
vertex.

Let
u
be the
Q
or
T
outgoing neighbor of
v
,

\mathsf{conf}(v)=\left\langle q,c_{1},c_{2}\right\rangle
, and

\mathsf{conf}(u)=\left\langle q^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime}\right\rangle
.
Note that
if
\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{zero}}_{i}(v)
,
then
c_{i}=0
;
if
\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,\delta}(v)
,
then
c^{\prime}_{i}-c_{i}=\delta
;
if
\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{j}(v)
,
then
q^{\prime}=j
.
We now establish a relationship between
\mathsf{conf}(v)
and
\mathsf{conf}(u)
, depending on the
q^{th}
instruction of
\mathcal{M}
.
Note that
\mathcal{G}\models\psi_{q}(v)
.

–

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{INC}\ (c_{i})
,
then

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{q+1}(v)
,

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,1}(v)
, and

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1-i,0}(v)
.
Thus

q^{\prime}=q+1
,
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}+1
, and
c^{\prime}_{1-i}=c_{1-i}
.
Hence
\mathsf{conf}(u)
is the successor configuration of
\mathsf{conf}(v)
.

–

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
\texttt{IF}\ (c_{i}=0)\ \texttt{GOTO}\ ({j})
,
if
c_{i}=0
,
then

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{j}(v)
,

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{0,0}(v)
, and

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1,0}(v)
.
Thus
q^{\prime}=j
,
c^{\prime}_{0}=c_{0}
, and
c^{\prime}_{1}=c_{1}
.
Otherwise, if
c_{i}>0
,
then

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{q+1}(v)
,

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{i,-1}(v)
, and

\mathcal{G}\models\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{1-i,0}(v)
.
Thus
q^{\prime}=q+1
,
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}-1
, and
c^{\prime}_{1-i}=c_{1-i}
.
Hence
\mathsf{conf}(u)
is the successor configuration of
\mathsf{conf}(v)
.

–

If the
q^{th}
instruction is
HALT
,
then
\mathcal{G}\models\left({\#_{y}\left[E(v,y)\land T(y)\right]}=1\right)
,
which implies that
u
is a
T
vertex.

We now claim that
\mathcal{M}
halts.
Our definition of graph requires the vertex set

V
to be nonempty.
For a vertex in
V
,
if it is not a
S
vertex,
then it has an
S
outgoing neighbor.
Therefore there is at least one
S
vertex in
V
.
Let
v_{s}
be an
S
vertex in
V
.
By the claim above,
it has exactly one outgoing neighbor
v_{1}
,
and for this node we have
\mathsf{conf}(v_{1})=\left\langle 1,0,0\right\rangle
.

We demonstrate that there exists a finite sequence
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{\ell+1}
,
where for
2\leq i\leq\ell
,
v_{i}
is a
Q
outgoing neighbor of
v_{i-1}
,
and
v_{\ell+1}
is a
T
outgoing neighbor of
v_{\ell}
.
Note that each
Q
vertex has exactly one
Q
or
T
outgoing neighbor.
If there were no such finite sequence, then the length of the maximal sequence of
Q
nodes starting from
v_{1}
is infinite. Then, since
\mathcal{G}
is a finite graph,
there must exist vertices
v_{j}
and
v_{j^{\prime}}
in the sequence such that
j\neq j^{\prime}
and
v_{j}=v_{j^{\prime}}
.
Without loss of generality, let
j
and
j^{\prime}
be the smallest such pair with
j<j^{\prime}
.
If
j=1
, then
v_{1}
would have two incoming neighbors
v_{s}
and
v_{j-1}
,
but
v_{1}
has only one incoming neighbor. Hence we have a contradiction.
If
j>1
, then
v_{j}
would have two incoming neighbors
v_{j-1}
and
v_{j^{\prime}-1}
,
which again leads to a contradiction. Thus we can always find such
T
vertex
v_{\ell+1}
.

By the claim above, for
2\leq i\leq\ell
,

\mathsf{conf}(v_{i})
is the successor configuration of
\mathsf{conf}(v_{i-1})
.
In addition,
\mathsf{conf}(v_{1})=\left\langle 1,0,0\right\rangle
and
\mathsf{conf}(v_{\ell})
is a halt configuration.
Therefore
\mathsf{conf}(v_{1}),\mathsf{conf}(v_{2}),\ldots,\mathsf{conf}(v_{\ell})

is a computation of
\mathcal{M}
. Because the length of the computation is finite,
\mathcal{M}
halts.

Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
halts
.
Let
\left\langle q_{0},c_{0,0},c_{1,0}\right\rangle,\cdots,\left\langle q_{\ell},c_{0,\ell},c_{1,\ell}\right\rangle
be the computation of
\mathcal{M}
.
We define the graph
\mathcal{G}
as follows.
Let
S:=\left\{v_{s}\right\}
,
S:=\left\{v_{t}\right\}
,

C_{0}:=\left\{v_{0,i,j}\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq j\leq c_{0,i}\right\}
,

C_{1}:=\left\{v_{1,i,j}\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq j\leq c_{1,i}\right\}
, and

Q_{j}:=\left\{v_{i}\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell,q_{i}=j\right\}
for
1\leq j\leq n
.
The set of vertices is
S\cup C_{0}\cup C_{1}\bigcup_{j\in{\left[n\right]}}Q_{j}
, and
the set of edges is

\displaystyle E\ :=

\displaystyle\left\{(v_{s},v_{1}),(v_{\ell},v_{t})\right\}\cup\left\{(v_{i},v_{i+1})\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell-1\right\}\cup

\displaystyle\left\{(v_{i},v_{0,i,j})\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq j\leq c_{0,i}\right\}\cup\left\{(v_{i},v_{1,i,j})\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\ell,1\leq j\leq c_{1,i}\right\}.

It is then straightforward to check that
\mathcal{G}
is a finite graph,
as depicted in Fig.
3
,
and that it is a model of
\forall x\ \Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
.

Since the halting problem of two-counter machines is undecidable,
and
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
formulas can be translted to
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s,
we obtain the undecidability of the universal satisfibility problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
, by reduction from
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
.

Theorem 6.26
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is undecidable.

6.4
Decidability of satisfiability for “modal” GNNs with unbounded activation functions

We can see that even simple unbounded activation functions produce unbounded spectra,
so the proof technique in the truncated case certainly will not work.

Remark 6.27
.

Even simple GNNs may have unbounded spectra.
For example, let
\mathcal{A}
be a
1
-layer
1
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined as follows:
the dimensions are
{d^{\left(0\right)}}={d^{\left(1\right)}}=1
;
the coefficient matrix
\mathbf{C}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero matrix;

\left(\mathbf{A}^{\left(1\right)}_{\text{out}}\right)_{1,1}=1
;
the bias vector
\mathbf{b}^{\left(1\right)}
is a zero vector.
It is not difficult to see that
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{1}(v)
is the number of out-neighbors of
v
.
Hence, the
1
-spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
is the set of natural numbers.

We present a decidability result for the “modal version”: aggregation over nodes connected by outgoing edges only, within a directed graph:

Theorem 6.28
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is
\mathsf{NEXP}
-complete,
and it becomes
\mathsf{NP}
-complete when the number of layers is fixed.

Analogously to what we did in the eventually constant case, we describe all the possible values of a given activation function.
Unlike in the eventually constant case, this will not be a finite set, but it will be
semi-linear
: that is, describable using a formula of Presburger arithmetic.
Furthermore, the this set is definable by a polynomial size
existential Presburger arithmetic with stars
(
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
) formula, which was studied by
[
12
]
.

Definition 6.29
.

We give the syntax of
existential Presburger arithmetic with stars
(
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
).

•

For
m,n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
\mathbf{x}
be a tuple of
n
variables,

\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m\times n}
, and
\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
,

\mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}\geq\mathbf{b}
is an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula.

•

If
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
and
\psi(\mathbf{y})

are
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formulas,
then so is

\varphi(\mathbf{x})\land\psi(\mathbf{y})
and

\varphi(\mathbf{x})\lor\psi(\mathbf{y})
.

•

If
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula and
\mathbf{y}\subseteq\mathbf{x}
,
then so is
\exists\mathbf{y}\ \varphi(\mathbf{x})
.

•

If
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula,
then so is
\psi(\mathbf{x}):=\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
.

The semantics of the linear inequalities, Boolean connectives, and existential quantifiers is as usual,
and the semantics of the Kleene star is defined as follows.
Considering
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\psi(\mathbf{x}):=\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
we say that
\psi(\mathbf{a})
holds if there exists

\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
such that

\varphi\left(\mathbf{a}_{t}\right)
holds and

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}\mathbf{a}_{i}
.

Definition 6.30
.

The
star height
of an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
is the depth of nesting of the Kleene star operator. It is zero for formulas that
have no Kleene star, and the star height of the formula
\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
is the star height
of the formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
plus 1.

Theorem 6.31

(
[
12
]
, Theorem III.1.)
.

The satisfiability problem of
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
is
\mathsf{NEXP}
-complete,
and it becomes
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for fixed star height.

We say that a set
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}^{n}
is defined by the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
with common denominator
D\in\mathbb{N}
if the following properties hold.

•

For every
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{C}
,
D\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

•

\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{C}
if and only if
\varphi(D\mathbf{a})
holds.

Definition 6.32
.

A
piecewise linear functions
is defined
by a finite sequence
(I_{1},f_{1}),\ldots,(I_{k},f_{k})

where
\bigcup_{t\in{\left[k\right]}}I_{t}
is a partition of
\mathbb{Q}
into
k
intervals
and each
f_{t}:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{Q}
is an affine transformation.
For every
q\in\mathbb{Q}
,

q
is mapped to
f_{t}(q)
if
q
is in the interval
I_{t}
.

For every piecewise linear function
f
and
D\in\mathbb{N}
,
it is not difficult to show that there exist an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\Phi_{f,D}(x,y)
,
called the
characteristic formula of
f
with common denominator
D
,
such that for every
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\Phi_{f,D}(p,q)
holds if and only if
q/D=f(p/D)
.

We now state our representation theorem, which immediately implies
the upper bound Theorem
6.28
.
Recall that given a GNN
\mathcal{A}
,
for graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
for
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
the
\ell
-history of
v
(w.r.t.
\mathcal{A}
), defined in Definition
5.2
,
is the tuple that collects the first
\ell+1
feature vectors of
v
.
The
\ell
-history-space
of
\mathcal{A}
,
denoted by
{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,
is the set that collects
\ell
-history over all possible graph and vertices.

We contrast the theorem with Theorem
4.2
. There we could only overapproximate the spectrum, because we could not determine which numbers from previously layers were simultaneously realizable.
By inductively maintaining the entire history at each node, we have enough information to resolve these questions of consistency, and compute an
exact
representation of the semantic object, not just an overapproximation.

Definition 6.33
.

For an
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
, the
common denominator of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted by
D_{\mathcal{A}}
,
is the least common denominator of denominator of coefficients matrices and bias vectors in
\mathcal{A}
and coefficients in piecewise linear activation functions.

Theorem 6.34
.

For every
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}
and
0\leq\ell\leq L
,
the
\ell
-history-space
{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
of
\mathcal{A}
is
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
definable with common denominator
D_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover, the size of the formula is polynomial in the description of
\mathcal{A}
,
and the star height of the formula is the number of layers of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof 6.35
.

We define the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)}
inductively.
For the base case
\ell=0
,

{\varphi^{\left(0\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)}\right)}\ :=\ \bigwedge_{i\in{\left[{d^{\left(0\right)}}\right]}}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)}_{i}=0\ \lor\ \mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)}_{i}=D_{\mathcal{A}}\right).

For
1\leq\ell\leq L
, let

\displaystyle\chi^{\left(t\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(t\right)},\mathbf{x}^{\left(t-1\right)},\mathbf{y}^{\left(t\right)}\right)\ :=

\displaystyle\exists\mathbf{z}^{\left(t-1\right)}\ \bigwedge_{i\in{\left[{d^{\left(t\right)}}\right]}}\Phi_{f^{\left(t\right)},D_{\mathcal{A}}}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\left(t\right)}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{\left(t\right)}_{i}\right)\ \land\

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathbf{z}^{\left(t\right)}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}=\mathbf{C}^{\left(t\right)}\left(\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\left(t-1\right)}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\right)+\mathbf{A}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\left(t-1\right)}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\right)+\mathbf{b}^{\left(t\right)}\right)

\displaystyle{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)}\ :=

\displaystyle\exists\mathbf{y}^{\left(0\right)}\cdots\mathbf{y}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\ \bigwedge_{t\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\chi^{\left(t\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(t\right)},\mathbf{x}^{\left(t-1\right)},\mathbf{y}^{\left(t-1\right)}\right)\ \land\

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\varphi^{\left(0\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)}\right)}\land\left(\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\left(\mathbf{y}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{y}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\right)\right)^{*},

where
\Phi_{f^{\left(\ell\right)},D_{\mathcal{A}}}(x,z)
is the characteristic formula of the piecewise linear function
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
with common denominator
D_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Note that the size of
{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)}
is polynomial in the description of
\mathcal{A}
,
and its star height is the number of layers of
\mathcal{A}
.

We now prove that
{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(\ell\right)}\right)}

defines
{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
with common denominator
D_{\mathcal{A}}
, by induction on
\ell
.
The base case
\ell=0
is trivial.
For the induction hypothesis,
we assume that the lemma holds for
\ell-1
.

Suppose that
\mathbf{h}\in{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
.
By the definition of
{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell\right)}}
,
there exists a graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v\in V
such that

\mathbf{h}=\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell\right)}(v)
.
For
0\leq t\leq\ell
, let

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}\ :=

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}[t]

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}\ :=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell\right)}(u)[t]

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}\ :=

\displaystyle D_{\mathcal{A}}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(t\right)}\mathbf{h}[t-1]+\mathbf{A}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell\right)}(u)[t-1]+\mathbf{b}^{\left(t\right)}\right).

It is obvious that the following relation between

\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}
,
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}
, and
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}
holds:

\frac{\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\ =\ \mathbf{C}^{\left(t\right)}\left(\frac{\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\right)+\mathbf{A}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}_{\text{out}}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}\right)+\mathbf{b}^{\left(t\right)}.

By the semantics of these GNNs, for
1\leq t\leq\ell
, it holds that

\mathbf{h}[t]\ =\ f^{\left(t\right)}\left(\mathbf{C}^{\left(t\right)}\mathbf{h}[t-1]+\mathbf{A}^{\left(t\right)}_{\text{out}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell\right)}(u)[t-1]+\mathbf{b}^{\left(t\right)}\right),

which implies that

\frac{\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}}{D_{\mathcal{A}}}=f^{\left(t\right)}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}/D_{\mathcal{A}}\right)
.
Since
\Phi_{f^{\left(\ell\right)},D_{\mathcal{A}}}(x,z)
is the characteristic formula of
f^{\left(\ell\right)}
with common denominator
D_{\mathcal{A}}
,
it follows that
\Phi_{f^{\left(\ell\right)},D_{\mathcal{A}}}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}_{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E},\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}\right)
holds.
Thus, it holds that
\chi^{\left(t-1\right)}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)},\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)},\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}_{\text{out}}\right)
.

By the induction hypothesis,
{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
is defined by

{\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\right)}
with common denominator
D_{\mathcal{A}}
.
For every
u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)
, note that
\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)\in{{\mathcal{H}}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}}
.
Therefore, we have

{\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\left(D_{\mathcal{A}}\xi^{\left(0\right)}(u),\ldots,D_{\mathcal{A}}\xi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}(u)\right)}
,
which implies that

\left({\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\left(\mathbf{b}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{b}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\right)}\right)^{*}
holds.
Thus, it holds that
{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}[0],\ldots,D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}[\ell]\right)}
.

For every
\mathbf{h}
satisfing
{\varphi^{\left(\ell\right)}\left(D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}[0],\ldots,D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}[\ell]\right)}
,
there exist
\mathbf{h}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{h}_{k}
,
such that, for
1\leq t\leq k
,

{\varphi^{\left(\ell-1\right)}\left(D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}_{t}[0],\ldots,D_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{h}_{t}[\ell-1]\right)}
holds.
By the induction hypothesis, there exists graph
\mathcal{G}_{t}
and vertex
v_{t}
,
such that
\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}_{t}}(v_{t})=\mathbf{h}_{t}
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be the graph obtained by taking the disjoint union of
\mathcal{G}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{k}

and adding a fresh vertex
v_{r}
.
We set the colors of
v_{r}
so that
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v_{r})=\mathbf{h}[0]
.
For
1\leq t\leq k
, there exists an edge from
v_{r}
to
v_{t}
.
It is routine to verify that the
\ell
-history of
v_{r}
is precisely
\mathbf{h}
.
Note that
because
\mathcal{A}
is outgoing-only, the edge from
v_{r}
to
v_{t}
has no effect on the
(\ell-1)
-history of
v_{t}
.
Thus
\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}}(v_{t})=\mathsf{hist}^{\left(\ell-1\right)}_{\mathcal{G}_{t}}(v_{t})
.

Corollary 6.36
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is in
\mathsf{NEXP}
,
and it becomes
\mathsf{NP}
when the number of layers is fixed.

Proof 6.37
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.
By Throrem
6.34
,
there exists the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
{\varphi^{\left(L\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)}\right)}

define the
L
-history-space of
\mathcal{A}
with common denominator of
\mathcal{A}
.
Combining the acceptance condition of GNNs,

\mathcal{A}
is satisfiable if and only if the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
sentence

\Phi\ :=\ \exists\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)}\cdots\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)},{\varphi^{\left(L\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)}\right)}\ \land\ \left(2\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)}_{1}\geq D_{\mathcal{A}}\right)

is satisfiable.
Note that we can construct
{\varphi^{\left(L\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)}\right)}
in polynomial time in the description of
\mathcal{A}
, and thus the same is true of
\Phi
.
Since the satisfiability problem of
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
is in
\mathsf{NEXP}
by Theorem
6.31
,
the satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is also in
\mathsf{NEXP}
.
The star height of
{\varphi^{\left(L\right)}\left(\mathbf{x}^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,\mathbf{x}^{\left(L\right)}\right)}
is the number of layers of
\mathcal{A}
, and thus the same holds for
\Phi
.
Therefore the satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is in
\mathsf{NP}
when the number of layers is fixed.

The hardness of the satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathcal{PW}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is by reducing
from the satisfiability problem of
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formulas,
which is known to be
\mathsf{NEXP}
-hard from Theorem
6.31
.
Note that fixed layer
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s can be simulated by fixed layer
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s with a linear blowup.
By Theorem
5.1
, the satisfiability problem of fixed layer
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard, and thus the same holds for fixed layer
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.

Theorem 6.38
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is
\mathsf{NEXP}
-hard.

The proof works by considering
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formulas in normal form.

Definition 6.39
.

We say that a quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is in
normal form

if it is either star-free or it is in the form
\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
,
where
\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x})
is quantifier-free and star-free while

\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x})
is a quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula in normal form

We say that an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is in
normal form

if it is in the form
\exists\mathbf{y}\ \psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
,
where
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is a quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula in
normal form
.

Informally, normal form requires the occurrences of Kleene star to be layered one on top of the other. So, for example, a formula of the form
\left(\phi_{1}(x)\right)^{*}\land\left(\phi_{2}(y)\right)^{*}
is
not
in
normal formula. We can rewrite it in normal form as:

\left((x=0\lor\phi_{1}(x))\land(y=0\lor\phi_{2}(y))\right)^{*}.

For translating to GNNs, this normal form allows us to conveniently map occurrences
of GNN to aggregations at different levels.

Lemma 6.40
.

There is a polynomial time algorithm that converts an
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
into a semantically equivalent
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula in normal form.

We say that a
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\left(\phi(\mathbf{z})\right)^{*}
is a top-level star subformula of the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
,
if there is no
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\left(\psi(\mathbf{y})\right)^{*}
satisfied that

\left(\phi(\mathbf{z})\right)^{*}
is a strict subformula
\left(\psi(\mathbf{y})\right)^{*}
and

\left(\psi(\mathbf{y})\right)^{*}
is a subformula of
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
.

Proof 6.41
.

We first observe that for every
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
,
the formulas
\exists\mathbf{y}\ \left(\varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)^{*}
and

\left(\exists\mathbf{y}\ \varphi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right)^{*}

are semantically equivalent.
Thus, with routine procedure, we assume that the formula is in the prefix normal form in any time.

Next, for a quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
with
m
top-level star subformulas

\left(\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*},\ldots,\left(\phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
.
We claim that
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
and
\exists\mathbf{x}_{1}\cdots\mathbf{x}_{m}\ \psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})

are semantically equivalent, where

\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m}
are fresh variables and

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})\ :=

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})\ \land\ \left(\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})\right)^{*}

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})\ :=

\displaystyle\varphi\left[\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{1}/\left(\phi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}\right]\ldots\left[\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{m}/\left(\phi_{m}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}\right]

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i\in{\left[m\right]}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{0}\lor\phi_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\right).

Suppose that
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
is satisfiable by
\mathbf{a}
.
For
1\leq i\leq m
, let

\mathbf{a}_{i}\ :=\ \begin{cases}\mathbf{a},&\text{if $\left(\psi(\mathbf{a})\right)^{*}$ holds}\\
\mathbf{0},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is not difficult to check that
\psi(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{m})
holds.

On the other hand,
suppose that
\psi(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{m})
holds.
For
1\leq i\leq m
,
by the definition of
\psi
,

\left(\phi_{i}(\mathbf{a}_{i})\right)^{*}
holds.
Therefore, if
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{a}_{i}
, then
\left(\psi_{i}(\mathbf{a})\right)^{*}
holds.
Note that negations are not allowed in
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula.
Since
\psi(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{m})
holds,
\varphi(\mathbf{a})
also holds.

Finally, note that the star height of the formula
\varphi_{2}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m})
is one less than the origin formula
\varphi(\mathbf{x})
.
We can repeat this procedure and obtain the desired formula in the normal form.

It is worth noting that the
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\exists\mathbf{y}\ \psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is satisfiable
if and only if
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is satisfiable.
Thus, it is sufficient to only consider quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formulas in the normal form.

We are now ready to prove the hardness of the satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.
Let
\Psi(\mathbf{x})
be a quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula in normal form with
n
variables and star height
k
,

\Psi(\mathbf{x}):=\psi_{k}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\psi_{k-1}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\cdots\land\left(\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}\right)^{*}\right)^{*},

where
\psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})
are quantifier-free and star-free,
Let
\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{x}):=\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})
, and,
for
1\leq t\leq k
,

\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=\ \psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\Psi_{t-1}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}.

Note that
\Psi(\mathbf{x})=\Psi_{k}(\mathbf{x})
.
For
0\leq t\leq k
,
let
\ell_{t}:=\sum_{i\in{\left[t\right]}}\left(20pt{\psi_{i}}+1\right)
.

We define the
(\ell_{k}+1)
-layer
n
-
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
as follows.
The coefficient matrices and bias vectors are chosen so that the features of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
will satisfy certain conditions.

For the first layer,

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
0,&\text{for $n<i\leq n+2$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=n+3$}.\end{cases}

For layers between
2
and
\ell_{0}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
test whether
\psi_{0}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(0\right)}(v)\right)
holds,
where
\mathbf{a}^{\left(0\right)}(v)
is the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)
.
That is

\xi^{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+3$}\\
{\left\llbracket\psi_{0}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(0\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+4$}.\end{cases}

For
1\leq t\leq k
,
for the
(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}})
layer,

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}-1\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}-1\right)}_{i+2}(u),&\text{for $n<i\leq n+2$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=n+3$}.\end{cases}

For layers between
\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}+1
and
\ell_{t}
,

\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
test whether
\psi_{t}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)
holds,
where
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)
is the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}(v)
.
That is

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+3$}\\
{\left\llbracket\psi_{t}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)\ \land\ \xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+1}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+2}(v)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+4$}.\end{cases}

Finally, for the last layer,
\xi^{\left(\ell_{k}+1\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell_{k}\right)}_{n+4}(v)
.
Note that, for
1\leq t\leq k
,

\ell_{t}-\ell_{t-1}=20pt{\psi_{t}}+1
.
Thus, these definitions cannot clash.
By Lemma
6.14
, it is easy to see that there are
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s that satisfy these conditions.

One direction of the theorem will follows once we have shown the following property of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
:

Lemma 6.42
.

For every quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\Psi(\mathbf{x})
,
let
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
be the
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined above.
For every
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
for
0\leq t\leq k
,
if
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{n+4}(v)=1
,
then
\Psi_{t}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)
holds,
where
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)
is the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}(v)
.

Proof 6.43
.

We prove the lemma by induction on the star height of the formula.
For the base case
t=0
, note that
\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})=\psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})
is quantifier-free and star-free.
Thus the property easily follows the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
.

For the induction step
1\leq t\leq k
,
we first observe that, for
1\leq i\leq n
,
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
,

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{i}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}-20pt{\psi_{t-1}}\right)}_{i}(u),

which implies that
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}(u)
.
Next, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
,
if
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{n+4}(v)=1
,
then following properties follow.

•

\psi_{t}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)
holds.

•

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+1}(v)=\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+2}(v)
.
Note that, by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
,

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+1}(v)\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{n+3}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}1

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}-20pt{\psi_{t}}\right)}_{n+2}(v)\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{n+4}(u),

which implies that, for every
u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)
,

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{n+4}(u)=1
.
By the induction hypothesis, it follows that

\Psi_{t-1}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}(u)\right)
holds.
Since
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)=\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v)}\mathbf{a}^{\left(t-1\right)}(u)
,
by the semantics of Kleene star,
\left(\Psi_{t-1}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)\right)^{*}
holds.

Combining above two results, we obtain that

\Psi_{t}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v)\right)
holds.

We now prove the other direction.

Lemma 6.44
.

For every quantifier-free
\exists\mathsf{PA}^{*}
formula
\Psi(\mathbf{x})
in normal form,
let
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
be the
\mathcal{O}\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
defined above.
If
\Psi(\mathbf{x})
is satisfiable, then
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
is satisfiable.

Proof 6.45
.

For
0\leq t\leq k
,
for every valid assignment
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
of
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})
,
we define the
n
-tree
{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)}
inductively.
For the base case
t=0
,
the set of vertices in
{\mathcal{T}\left(0,\mathbf{a}\right)}
is
\left\{v_{r}\right\}\cup\left\{v_{i}\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\tilde{a}\right\}
,
where
\tilde{a}
is the maximum entry of
\mathbf{a}
.
For
1\leq i\leq n
,
U_{t}=\left\{v_{j}\middle|\ 1\leq j\leq a_{i}\right\}
,
where
a_{i}
is the
i^{th}
entries of
\mathbf{a}
.
The edges are
E=\left\{(v_{r},v_{i})\middle|\ 1\leq i\leq\tilde{a}\right\}
.

For the inductive step
1\leq t\leq k
,
recall that
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})=\psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\Psi_{t-1}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
.
Since
\mathbf{a}
is a valid assignment of
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})
,
there exists
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
satisfying that

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\mathbf{a}_{i}
and

\Psi_{t-1}\left(\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)
holds.
The
n
-tree
{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)}
is a disjoint union of

{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}
and a fresh vertex
v_{r}
.
For
1\leq i\leq\ell
, there exists an edge from
v_{r}
to
v_{i}
, where

v_{i}
is the root of
{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}
.

The correctness of the construction follows from the following claim:
For
0\leq t\leq k

for every valid assignment
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
of
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})
,
it holds that

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+4}(v_{r})=t-1\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v_{r})=\mathbf{a},

where
v_{r}
is the root of
{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)}

and
\mathbf{a}^{\left(t\right)}(v_{r})
consists of the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+4}(v_{r})
.

We prove the claim by induction on the star height of
\Psi(\mathbf{x})
.
For the base case
t=0
,
by the definition of
{\mathcal{T}\left(0,\mathbf{a}\right)}
,
the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(0,\mathbf{a}\right)}}(v_{r})

are exactly
\mathbf{a}
.
Since
\mathbf{a}
is a valid assignment of
\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})
,
it holds that

\xi^{\left(\ell_{0}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(0,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+4}(v_{r})\ =\ {\left\llbracket\Psi_{0}\left(\mathbf{a}^{\left(0\right)}(v_{r})\right)\right\rrbracket}\ =\ {\left\llbracket\Psi_{0}\left(\mathbf{a}\right)\right\rrbracket}\ =\ 1.

For the inductive step
1\leq t\leq k
,
recall that
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})=\psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})\land\left(\Psi_{t-1}(\mathbf{x})\right)^{*}
.
Let
v_{r}
be the root of
{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)}
.
Since
\mathbf{a}
is a valid assignment of
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x})
,
there exists
\mathbf{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{a}_{\ell}
satisfying that

\mathbf{a}=\sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\mathbf{a}_{i}
and

\Psi_{t-1}\left(\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)
holds.
Let
v_{i}
be the root of
{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}
.
Note that since
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
is outgoing-only,
the edge from
v_{r}
to
v_{i}
did not affect the feature of
v_{i}
.
Thus, it holds that

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}}(v_{i})\ =\ \xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}}(v_{i}).

•

For
1\leq i\leq\ell
,
by the induction hypothesis,
the first
n
entries of
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)}}(v_{i})
are exactly
\mathbf{a}_{i}
.
By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
,
for
1\leq j\leq n
, it follows that

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},j}(v_{r})\ =\ \sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},j}(u)\ =\ \sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)},j}(v_{i}),

which implies that the first
n
entries of

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)}}(v_{r})
are
\mathbf{a}
.

•

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\Psi}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+1}(v_{r})\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+3}(u)\ =\ \sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)},n+3}(v_{i})\ =\ \sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}1

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+2}(v_{r})\ =

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}_{\text{out}}(v_{r})}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+4}(u)\ =\ \sum_{i\in{\left[\ell\right]}}\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)},n+4}(v_{i}).

For
1\leq i\leq\ell
,
by the induction hypothesis,
ite holds that
\xi^{\left(\ell_{t-1}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t-1,\mathbf{a}_{i}\right)},n+4}(v_{i})=1
.
Thus, we have

\xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+1}(v_{r})\ =\ \xi^{\left(\ell_{t}\right)}_{{\mathcal{T}\left(t,\mathbf{a}\right)},n+2}(v_{r}).

7
Discussion

This work extends the exploration of the relationship between aggregate-combine GNNs and logic, with exact characterizations of expressiveness for GNNs with eventually constant activation functions, and embedding a logic into the GNNs with standard
\mathsf{ReLU}
activations.
We also obtain both decidability and undecidability results, some using the logical characterizations and some by porting the techniques used for decidability of the logics to apply directly on the GNNs. Perhaps the main take-away, echoing the theme
of
[
2
]
, is that Presburger logics and the techniques for analyzing them can be relevant to GNNs.

We give a fairly comprehensive picture of the complex of satisfiability and universal satisfiability:
see Table
3.1
and
3.1
. We leave open a couple cases, such as truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
, outgoing-only, with global readout.

In our work we leave open the question of getting an exact logical characterization of the expressiveness
of GNNs with regular
\mathsf{ReLU}
. We presented a logic that was contained in this class, which was sufficient
to obtain negative results on verification.
Also note that in this work we considered GNNs with bounded input features. We believe our techniques extend to the case of graphs where the features are attributes with unbounded precision: we are examining this in ongoing work.

Lastly, note that our complexity analysis is rather limited. We provide complexity bounds for the outgoing-only case with truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
, and for GNNs based on truncated
\mathsf{ReLU}
and local aggregation, we have shown satisfiability is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete, and is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for a fixed number of layers. Of course, for the broad class of GNNs with eventually constant activation functions, it is difficult to talk about complexity bounds. But for specific eventually constant functions one could hope to say more.

Looking at broader open issues, we focused here on some very basic verification problems on GNNs: can a certain classification be achieved?
But it is clear that our techniques apply to many other logic-based verification problems; for example, it can be applied to determine whether a GNN can achieve a certain classification on a graph satisfying a certain sentence – provided that the sentence is also in one of our decidable logics.
In ongoing work, we are developing verification tools based on the ideas in the paper, via reduction to problems that can be decided using existing solvers.

Our work provides motivation for exploring the properties of Presburger logics over relational structures and their connections with GNNs beyond the setting here, which considers only graphs with discrete feature values from a fixed set. In our ongoing work we are adapting our techniques to deal with GNNs whose feature values are
unbounded integers
, specified by an initial semi-linear set.

In the body of the paper, we have worked with a directed graph model. As mentioned earlier, all of our
results which will deal with bidirectional GNNs also apply to undirected graphs: we explain this in the appendix.

Appendix A

We summarize the results of the complexity and decidability of verification problems for GNNs over undirected graphs in Table
A
and Table
A
.
In cases where we have only written “decidable’, we have not computed precise bounds: and in the “eventually constant” case we would require more hypotheses in order to compute such bounds.
