A.1
Undirected decidability results for Section
4.1

In the body of the paper, we mentioned that the decidability results for GNNs with eventually constant activations also apply to undirected graphs. Recall that we consider an undirected graph as a directed graph where the edge relation is symmetrical.
We now explain the modifications needed to adapt the results in Section
4.1
to the undirected case.

We first prove the undirected version of Corollary
3.14
.
The main idea is that we can enforce undirectedness within the larger decidable logic
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
to obtain decidability:

Corollary A.1
.

The finite satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
over undirected graphs is decidable.

Proof A.2
.

The proof is similar to that of Corollary
3.14
.
Let
\varphi(x)
be an
n
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula and
U_{n+1}
be a fresh unary predicate.
We claim that
\varphi(x)
is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs if and only if the
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
sentence

\psi^{\prime}\ :=\ \psi\land\left(\forall x\ \forall y\ E(x,y)\to E(y,x)\right)

is also finitely satisfiable,
where
\psi:=\exists x\ U_{n+1}(x)\land\varphi(x)
is the
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
sentence defined in Corollary
3.14
.
Then the corollary follows from the decidability of the finite satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{GP^{2}}
by Theorem
3.13
.

If
\varphi(x)
is finitely satisfiable by the undirected
n
-graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v\in G
,
let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the
(n+1)
-graph that extended
\mathcal{G}
with
U_{n+1}:=\left\{v\right\}
.
By the argument similar to Corollary
3.14
,
we have
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\psi
.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is a undirected graph, for every vertices
v,u\in V
,
if
(v,u)\in E
, then
(u,v)\in E
,
which implies that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\forall x\ \forall y\ E(x,y)\to E(y,x)
.
Hence,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\psi^{\prime}
.

If
\psi
is finitely satisfiable by the
(n+1)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
,
let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the
n
-graph that restricted
\mathcal{G}
by removing
U_{n+1}
.
By the argument similar to Corollary
3.14
,
there exists a vertex
v\in V
such that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\models\varphi(v)
.
Since
\mathcal{G}\models\forall x\ \forall y\ E(x,y)\to E(y,x)
,
for every vertices
v,u\in V
,
if
(v,u)\in E
, then
(u,v)\in E
.
Therefore
\mathcal{G}
is a undirected graph, and so is
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

Recall that in Section
4.1
we obtained decidability results by reducing to satisfiability of the logic
over directed graphs.
It is now straightforward to show that by reducing to decidability in the logic
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
over undirected graphs, shown above,
we extend the results to the undirected case. That is, satisfiability and universal satisfiability problem for local GNNs with eventually constant activations over undirected graphs – that is, the standard notion of GNN – is decidable.

Theorem A.3
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is decidable.

Theorem A.4
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is decidable.

A.2
Undirected variants of Section
4.2
: undecidability in the eventually constantt case

We can also revise our undecidability results for global GNNs with eventually constant activations to the undirected case,
thus giving undecidability for the usual notion of GNN with global readout.
This is done with the same reduction from solvability of simple equation systems to the finite satisfiability of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas, which we can show works over undirected graphs.
The main difference is the following stronger version of Lemma
4.25
.

Lemma A.5
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
there exists an
(n+m)
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)

such that the following are equivalent,

1.

The system
\varepsilon
has a solution in
\mathbb{N}
.

2.

There exists an undirected
(n+m)
-graph
\mathcal{G}
such that for every vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
,
\mathcal{G}\models\Psi_{\varepsilon}(v)
.

3.

The formula
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs.

Proof A.6
.

Let
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x)
be the
(n+m)
-
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula which is defined in Lemma
4.25
.
The proof for (2)
\Rightarrow
(3) and (3)
\Rightarrow
(1) are the same.
We only need to revise the proof for (1)
\Rightarrow
(2).
Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by adding symmetric edges to
\mathcal{G}
in the proof.
Clearly,
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is undirected.
It can be verified that all arguments for
\mathcal{G}
also holds for
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Thus, the property holds trivially.

The following undecidability results over undirected graphs can be proven based on Lemma
A.5
.

Theorem A.7
.

The finite satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{MP^{2}}
over undirected graphs is undecidable.

Theorem A.8
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is undecidable.

Theorem A.9
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is undecidable.

A.3
Undirected
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness results for Section
5

We can also revise our
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-completeness results for local GNNs with
\mathsf{TrReLU}
to the undirected case:

Theorem A.10
.

The satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{TrReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete,
and it becomes
\mathsf{NP}
-complete when the number of layers is fixed.

The revisions for
\mathsf{PSPACE}
algorithm and
\mathsf{NP}
-complete results are obvious.
Here we only show the
\mathsf{PSPACE}
lower bound to the undirected case.

The idea of the proof is similar to Lemma
5.20
.
We establishe the
\mathsf{PSPACE}
lower bound
by reducing the concept satisfiability of the description logic
\mathcal{ALC}

into the satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
over undirected graphs.
We introduce fresh unary predicates
P_{0},\ldots,P_{L}
, and
enforce that the vertex with depth
\ell
only realizes
P_{\ell}
.
Thus, it is routine to convert models of the
\mathcal{ALC}
concept to the
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula and vice versa.

Lemma A.11
.

There exists a polynomial time translation
\pi
from
\mathcal{ALC}
concepts with one role
R
to
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formulas such that the
\mathcal{ALC}
concept
C
is satisfiable if and only if the
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\pi(C)
is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs.

Proof A.12
.

Suppose that the quantifier depth of the
\mathcal{ALC}
concept
C
is
L
.
The vocabulary of the
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
formula
\pi_{x}(C)
is the vocabulary of the
\mathcal{ALC}
concept
C
extended with fresh unary predicates
P_{0},\ldots,P_{L}
.
For
0\leq\ell\leq L
, let

\phi^{\left(\ell\right)}(x)\ :=\ \left(\bigwedge_{i\in{\left[0,\ell-1\right]}}\neg P_{i}(x)\right)\land P_{\ell}(x)\land\left(\bigwedge_{i\in{\left[\ell-1,L\right]}}\neg P_{i}(x)\right).

We will define translations
\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}
and
\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}
inductively, which are the standard translation from
\mathcal{ALC}
concepts to first-order logic formulas, except with some slight modification to quantifiers to fit our logic and guarantee the depth of elements in its tree models.
Note that the base case of the inductive definition is
\pi^{\left(L\right)}_{x}
and
\pi^{\left(L\right)}_{x}
.
Since the quantifier depth of the
\mathcal{ALC}
concept is
L
,
for the translations
\pi^{\left(L\right)}_{x}
and
\pi^{\left(L\right)}_{x}
,
there is no case for quantifiers.
The rules for the translations are

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(A)\ =

\displaystyle A(x)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(A)\ =

\displaystyle A(y)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(\neg C)\ =

\displaystyle\neg\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(C)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(\neg C)\ =

\displaystyle\neg\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(C)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(C\sqcap D)\ =

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(C)\land\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(D)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(C\sqcap D)\ =

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(C)\land\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(D)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(C\sqcup D)\ =

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(C)\lor\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(D)

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(C\sqcup D)\ =

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(C)\lor\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(D)

and

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(\exists R.C)\ =

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\phi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}(y)\land\pi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}_{y}(C)\right]}\geq 1

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(\exists R.C)\ =

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{x}\left[E(y,x)\land\phi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}(x)\land\pi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}_{x}(C)\right]}\geq 1

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{x}(\forall R.C)\ =

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land\phi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}(y)\land\neg\pi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}_{y}(C)\right]}=0

\displaystyle\pi^{\left(\ell\right)}_{y}(\forall R.C)\ =

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{x}\left[E(y,x)\land\phi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}(x)\land\neg\pi^{\left(\ell+1\right)}_{x}(C)\right]}=0.

Finally, we define
\pi(C):=\phi^{\left(0\right)}(x)\land\pi^{\left(0\right)}_{x}(C)
.

A.4
Undirected results for Section
6.1
: undecidability and separation for GNNs with unbounded activations

We now briefly explain why the undecidability and expressiveness separation results for GNNs with unbounded activation functions also apply to undirected graphs.

For the expressiveness results, recall that the key idea relies on the property:
“the number of two-hop paths from the vertex
v
to the green vertices is the same as the number of two-hop paths from the vertex
v
to the blue vertices.”
The separation is proved by constructing a sequence of pairs of bipolar graphs.
To extend this to undirected graphs, we define an “undirected” version of bipolar graphs by adding symmetric edges to the standard definition of bipolar graphs.
It is then routine to verify that all arguments hold for this undirected version of bipolar graphs.
Therefore, we obtain the following results for undirected graphs.

Lemma A.13
.

\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
is strictly more expressive over undirected graphs than
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.

Corollary A.14
.

\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
is strictly more expressive over undirected graphs than
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{MP^{2}}
.

Corollary A.15
.

\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s are strictly more expressive over undirected graphs than
\mathcal{L}\mathcal{C}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.

A.5
Undirected results for Section
6.2

Next, we turn to the satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s.
For a simple equation system
\varepsilon
, we say that
\varepsilon
has a non-trivial solution
if it has a solution where not all variables are zero.
For an undirected graph
\mathcal{G}
and a vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
, let
\mathcal{N}(v)
be the set of neighbors of
v
.

Lemma A.16
.

For every simple equation system
\varepsilon
with
n
variables and
m
equations,
there exists an
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}

such that

\varepsilon
has a non-trivial solution in
\mathbb{N}
if and only if

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable over undirected graphs.

Proof A.17
.

The proof is similar to Lemma
6.18
.

We define the 20-layer
(n+2m)
-
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
with

integer
coefficient matrices and bias vectors as follows.
The dimensions, coefficient matrices, and bias vectors will be chosen so that the features of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
satisfy the following conditions.
Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}x_{t}\geq 1

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})\ \land\ \left(x_{2n+2m+1}=1\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{t\in{\left[m\right]}}\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ \land\ \left(x_{n+2m+1}=x_{n+2m+2}\right)\ \land\ \Psi_{0}(\mathbf{x}),

where, for
1\leq t\leq m
,

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=1
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and
\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=1\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}+\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then
\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\top
and

\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x}):=\left(x_{t_{1}}=x_{t_{2}}+x_{t_{3}}\right)
.

•

if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then

\displaystyle\Psi_{1,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(\left(x_{n+t}=0\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=0\right)\right)\lor\left(\left(x_{n+t}=x_{n+2m+t_{2}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2,t}(\mathbf{x})\ :=

\displaystyle\left(x_{n+t}=x_{t_{1}}\right)\land\left(x_{n+m+t}=x_{t_{3}}\right).

For the first layer,

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+m$}\\
\xi^{\left(0\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $n+m<i\leq n+2m$}.\end{cases}

For the second to the fifth layers,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test whether
\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m$}\\
{\left\llbracket\psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+2m+1$}.\end{cases}

For the sixth layer,

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{i-n-2m}(u),&\text{for $n+2m<i\leq 2n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{i-n}(u),&\text{for $i=2n+2m+1$}\\
1,&\text{for $i=2n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

For the seventh to the fourteenth layers,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test whether
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is,

\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq 2n+2m+2$}\\
{\left\llbracket\psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=2n+2m+3$}.\end{cases}

For the fifteenth layer,

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{i}(u),&\text{for $n<i\leq n+2m$}\\
\sum_{u\in\mathcal{N}(v)}\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{i+n+1}(u),&\text{for $n+2m<i\leq n+2m+2$}.\end{cases}

For the sixteenth to the nineteenth layers,

\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
test whether
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(15\right)}(v)\right)
holds.
That is

\xi^{\left(19\right)}_{i}(v)\ =\ \begin{cases}\xi^{\left(19\right)}_{i}(v),&\text{for $1\leq i\leq n+2m+2$}\\
{\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(15\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket},&\text{for $i=n+2m+3$}.\end{cases}

Finally, for the last layer,
\xi^{\left(20\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(19\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)
.
By Lemma
6.14
, it is easy to see that there are
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s that satisfy these conditions.

We now show that this construction has the required properties for the lemma.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfiable
by the graph
\mathcal{G}
and vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}
.
Let
a_{i}:=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)
.
We will show that
\left\{\upsilon_{i}\leftarrow a_{i}\right\}_{i\in{\left[n\right]}}
is a solution of
\varepsilon
.

The proof idea is similar to Lemma
6.18
,
with the only difference being the following variant of the main claim used in that lemma:

The following properties hold for every
u\in\mathcal{N}(v)
.

•

For
1\leq i\leq n
,

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+2m+i}(u)=a_{i}
.

•

For
1\leq t\leq m
,
if the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
,
then

\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

We now prove the claim.
By the argument similar to Lemma
6.18
.
For every
u\in\mathcal{N}(v)
,

\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(u)\right)
holds.

•

By the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
, it holds that

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{2n+2m+1}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(u)}\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{n+2m+1}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(u)}{\left\llbracket\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(u^{\prime})\right)\right\rrbracket}.

Because
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(u)\right)
holds,
it follows that
\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{2n+2m+1}(u)=1
.
This implies that
there is only one neighbor
\widetilde{u}
of
u

for which
\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(\widetilde{u})\right)
holds.
Therefore,
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
for every
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(u)
with
u^{\prime}\neq\widetilde{u}
,

\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(u^{\prime})=0
.
Thus, for
1\leq i\leq n
,
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
,
it holds that

\displaystyle\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+2m+i}(u)\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(u)}\xi^{\left(5\right)}_{i}(u^{\prime})\ =\ \sum_{u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(u)}\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(u^{\prime})\ =\ \xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(\widetilde{u}).

Once we show that
\widetilde{u}=v
,
it follows that

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+2m+i}(u)=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(\widetilde{v})=a_{i}
.

Since
v
is a neighbor of
u
and

\widetilde{u}
is the only neighbor of
u

for which
\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(\widetilde{u})\right)
holds.
To show that
\widetilde{u}=v
,
it is sufficient to demonstrate that
\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)
holds.

Because
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
is satisfied by
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v\right\rangle
,

\xi^{\left(20\right)}_{1}(v)=\xi^{\left(19\right)}_{n+2m+3}(v)={\left\llbracket\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(15\right)}(v)\right)\right\rrbracket}=1
.
Since
\Psi_{2}\left(\xi^{\left(15\right)}(v)\right)
holds,

\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(15\right)}(v)\right)
also holds.
Note that, for
1\leq i\leq n
,
by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
,

\xi^{\left(15\right)}_{i}(v)=\xi^{\left(1\right)}_{i}(v)
.
Hence
\Psi_{0}\left(\xi^{\left(1\right)}(v)\right)
holds.

•

Fix
1\leq t\leq n
, and suppose that
the
t^{th}
equation in
\varepsilon
is
\upsilon_{t_{1}}=\upsilon_{t_{2}}\cdot\upsilon_{t_{3}}
.
Since
\Psi_{1}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(u)\right)
holds,

\Psi_{1,t}\left(\xi^{\left(6\right)}(u)\right)
also holds.
Thus, one of the following must hold:

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+t}(u)=0\land\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=0\quad\text{or}\quad\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+t}(u)=\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+2m+t_{2}}(u)\land\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=1.

Note that by the definition of
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
,

\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+t}(u)\ =\ \xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+t}(u)\quad\text{and}\quad\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)=\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+m+t}(u).

By the previous claim,

\xi^{\left(6\right)}_{n+2m+t_{2}}(u)=a_{t_{2}}
.
Therefore, for both cases,

\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+t}(u)=a_{t_{2}}\cdot\xi^{\left(14\right)}_{n+m+t}(u)
.

Suppose
\varepsilon
is solvable
.
Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be the graph obtained by adding symmetric edges to the directed graph
\mathcal{G}
constructed in the proof of Lemma
6.16
.
We can show that
\mathcal{A}_{\varepsilon}
accepts
\left\langle\mathcal{G},v_{0}\right\rangle

via an argument similar to the one used in Lemma
6.16
.

Since the solvability (over
\mathbb{N}
) of simple equation systems is undecidable,
we obtain undecidability of the satisfiability problems for
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
.

Theorem A.18
.

The satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is undecidable.

A.6
Undirected results for Section
6.3

To obtain undecidability of the universal satisfiability problem for
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
over undirected graphs,
we again reduce from two-counter machines, but now with a modification to guarantee the direction of the transition of the counter machine.

Lemma A.19
.

For every two-counter machine
\mathcal{M}
with
n
instructions, there exists an
(n+8)
-
\mathcal{L}{\text{-}}\mathsf{M2P^{2}}
formula
\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
such that
\mathcal{M}
halts if and only if
\forall x\ \Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs.

We cannot apply the exact encoding from Figure
3
and Lemma
6.24
here,
because in that encoding we distinguished the predecessor and successor configurations using the direction of edges.
Here we sketch the trick used to overcome the lack of direction in the edges.
We will utilize the predicates from the proof of Lemma
6.24
. In particular
we will have a predicate
Q
and an associated notion of
Q
vertex as in that proof.

We introduce three fresh unary predicates
I_{0}
,
I_{1}
, and
I_{2}
to label the configuration’s index modulo
3
.
We add an extra clause to the formula to guarantee that each element has exactly one of these three index labels.
Each
C_{0}
or
C_{1}
vertex has exactly the same index as the
Q
vertex that connected to it.
Finally, for each
Q
vertex
v
with index
i
,
there exists at most one
Q
vertex
v^{\prime}
with index
(i+1\bmod 3)
,
such that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are connected;
there exists at most one
Q
vertex
v^{\prime\prime}
with index
(i-1\bmod 3)
,
such that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are connected.
Therefore we can modify the formula which identifies the successor and predecessor
based on the index, rather than the direction of the edges, and show that the two-counter machine halts if and only if the modified formula is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs.

S
Q_{1}
Q_{2}
Q_{3}
Q_{8}
\cdots
Q_{7}
T
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{1}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{0}
C_{0}
I_{0}
I_{1}
I_{2}
I_{0}
I_{(i\bmod 3)}

Figure 4:
An example of the encoding of the computation of a two-counter machines in undirected graphs.

Formally the reduction is as follows.
Given a two-counter machine
\mathcal{M}
,
we construct the formula
\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)
as in the proof of
Lemma
6.24
.
Here we demonstrate only the main idea of the modification;
other formulas can be treated analogously.

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{diff}}_{t,\delta}(x)\ :=\ \bigwedge_{i\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty 2\right]}}I_{i}(x)\to(

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{z,y}\left[E(x,z)\land E(z,y)\land C_{t}(y)\land I_{(i+1\bmod 3)}(y)\right]}-

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land C_{t}(y)\land I_{i}(y)\right]}=\delta)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mathsf{succ}}_{j}(x)\ :=\ \bigwedge_{i\in{\left[0\mathinner{,}\penalty 2\right]}}I_{i}(x)\to(

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\#_{y}\left[E(x,y)\land Q_{j}(y)\land I_{(i+1\bmod 3)}(y)\right]}=1)

The proof that
\mathcal{M}
halts if and only if
\forall x\Psi_{\mathcal{M}}(x)

is finitely satisfiable over undirected graphs is similar to
the one for Lemma
6.24
,
thus, omitted.

Theorem A.20
.

The universal satisfiability problem of
\mathcal{L}\mathsf{ReLU}\mathsf{{\text{-}}GNN}
s over undirected graphs is undecidable.

The theorem follows from Lemma
A.19
, using the undecidability result for two-counter machines, as in Theorem
6.26
.
